Topic: a story about an unusual holiday trip

Text:
By Mark Thompson

Last summer, I decided to try something different for my holiday. Instead of going to the beach, I visited a small village in the mountains. The owner of a guesthouse there had an informal suggestion: "Walk up the hill at sunset—it’s magical!"

I followed his method and climbed slowly. Halfway up, my stomach hurt because I wasn’t used to the altitude. But when I reached the top, the view was amazing! The owner was right—it was worth the effort.

Normally, I don’t like hiking, but this trip changed my mind. I even brought my own camera to take photos. The best part? The owner let me stay inside his house to rest after the walk.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Mark decide to climb the hill?
Correct: Because the guesthouse owner suggested it and promised it would be beautiful.
Distractors: Because he wanted to test his fitness. / Because he had nothing else to do in the village. / Because his friends dared him to do it.
Explanation: The text says, "The owner of a guesthouse there had an informal suggestion: 'Walk up the hill at sunset—it’s magical!'" and "The owner was right—it was worth the effort." This shows Mark climbed because of the owner’s suggestion and promise of beauty. The other options are not supported by the text—there is no mention of fitness tests, boredom, or friends daring him.
---
Q2: What problem did Mark face during the climb?
Correct: He had stomach pain because of the altitude.
Distractors: He got lost on the way up. / He slipped and fell. / He ran out of water.
Explanation: The text states, "Halfway up, my stomach hurt because I wasn’t used to the altitude." This directly answers the question. The other options are incorrect because the text does not mention getting lost, falling, or running out of water.
---
Q3: What did Mark do after reaching the top of the hill?
Correct: He rested inside the owner’s house.
Distractors: He called his friends to share the view. / He cooked dinner with the owner. / He slept outside under the stars.
Explanation: The text says, "The best part? The owner let me stay inside his house to rest after the walk." This confirms the correct answer. The other options are unsupported—there is no mention of calling friends, cooking, or sleeping outside.



Topic: a story about an unusual family celebration

Text:
By Mark Thompson

Every year, my family celebrates our traditions in a completely unusual way. Instead of a big party, we have a "Silent Dinner." His idea was to eat together without talking—just enjoying the variety of food and the soft light from candles. At first, my sister and I argued about it, but now we love it!

After dinner, we write short essays about our favorite memories. Last year, my brother wrote about a trip to the mountains, and I described a deep conversation with my grandfather. Neither of us expected it to become our favorite tradition, but it has changed our family direction for the better.

Questions:
Q1: What is the main difference between the family’s celebration and a normal party?
Correct: They eat in silence and write essays about memories.
Distractors: They only eat food prepared by one person. / They celebrate outdoors under the stars. / They argue about traditions before starting.
Explanation: The text explains that the family eats in silence and writes essays, making the correct answer clear. The distractors are incorrect because the text does not mention food prepared by one person, outdoor celebrations, or arguing before the event.
---
Q2: Why did the family start this unusual tradition?
Correct: It was the father’s idea.
Distractors: The children insisted on it. / The family wanted to try something new after a disagreement. / The tradition came from a book the mother read.
Explanation: The text states, "His idea was to eat together without talking," meaning the father suggested it. The other options are incorrect because the text does not mention children insisting, a disagreement, or a book.
---
Q3: What did the brother and sister write about in their essays?
Correct: Personal memories.
Distractors: Their favorite foods. / Problems they had with their family. / A trip to the city.
Explanation: The text says, "my brother wrote about a trip to the mountains, and I described a deep conversation with my grandfather," showing they wrote about memories. The other options are incorrect because the text does not mention food, problems, or a city trip.



Topic: a description of a morning routine that makes me happy

Text:
By Maria Lopez

My morning routine is simple but makes my heart happy. First, I wake up early and go outside to the lake near my house. The fresh air and quiet water always give me a feeling of peace. Next, I take a general walk around the lake—it’s my favorite step of the day.

After that, I drink coffee while watching the sunrise. Sometimes, I see a celebrity jogging nearby, and I say, “Wow!” But even if I don’t, the view is amazing. Finally, I do light exercise to protect my health. By the time I reach home, I feel all right and ready for the day.

Questions:
Q1: What does Maria do first after waking up?
Correct: She goes outside to the lake.
Distractors: She drinks coffee. / She does exercise. / She watches the sunrise.
Explanation: The text says, “First, I wake up early and go outside to the lake near my house.” The correct answer is “She goes outside to the lake.” The other options are later steps in her routine: coffee comes after the walk, exercise is last, and sunrise is part of her coffee time.
---
Q2: Why does Maria say “Wow!” during her morning?
Correct: She sees a celebrity jogging.
Distractors: She admires the lake’s beauty. / She hears birds singing. / She feels relaxed.
Explanation: The text states, “Sometimes, I see a celebrity jogging nearby, and I say, ‘Wow!’” The correct answer is “She sees a celebrity jogging.” The other options are not mentioned—she doesn’t say “wow” for beauty, birds, or relaxation.
---
Q3: What does Maria do to feel ready for the day?
Correct: She does light exercise.
Distractors: She talks to neighbors. / She writes in a journal. / She takes a nap.
Explanation: The text says, “Finally, I do light exercise to protect my health.” The correct answer is “She does light exercise.” The other options are not part of her routine: neighbors, journaling, or napping are never mentioned.



Topic: a funny story that happened to my friends and me

Text:
By Mark Johnson

Last summer, my friends and I went camping in the mountains. We chose a small tent for four people, but it was a twin tent—only two sleeping areas! My friend Jake argued that we needed a bigger one, but we didn’t listen.

On the second night, we cooked dinner together. I burned the sweets (I meant to cook marshmallows!) and made a mess. My friend Leo joked, “You’re more like a celebrity chef than a real cook!” Everyone laughed, even though the food was terrible.

The next morning, we found a plate with only half a sandwich. We all chatted about who could have eaten it, but no one confessed. Then, we saw a double footprint in the mud—one of us must have slept outside! The character of our trip increased when we realized we were all tired, hungry, and still laughing.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Jake want a bigger tent?
Correct: Because the twin tent was too small for four people.
Distractors: Because he didn’t like the mountain view. / Because he wanted to sleep alone. / Because the tent was broken.
Explanation: The text says, “We chose a small tent for four people, but it was a twin tent—only two sleeping areas!” Jake argued for a bigger tent because it was too small. The other options are not mentioned in the text.
---
Q2: What happened to the marshmallows?
Correct: Mark burned them by mistake.
Distractors: They were stolen by an animal. / They were too sweet to eat. / They disappeared during the night.
Explanation: The text states, “I burned the sweets (I meant to cook marshmallows!)” This means Mark accidentally burned them. The other options are not supported by the text.
---
Q3: What made the trip funnier?
Correct: The fact that they were tired, hungry, and still laughing.
Distractors: Finding a celebrity’s autograph in the tent. / Discovering a hidden treasure in the mud. / Seeing a double footprint in the mud.
Explanation: The text says, “The character of our trip increased when we realized we were all tired, hungry, and still laughing.” The funny part was their silly situation, not the footprint (though it was funny too, it wasn’t the main reason). The other options are not mentioned.



Topic: an adventure story about getting lost in the forest

Text:
By Jake Carter

Last summer, my brother and I went hiking in the forest as a gift from our parents. It was a beautiful season, but we got lost. We had a map and a compass, but the trees were too thick, and we couldn’t find the network of trails. My brother was nervous, but I told him not to worry—we just need to stay calm.

We checked our equipment and saw we had water, snacks, and a flashlight. My brother dropped his sock in the mud, and we laughed, even though we were scared. After walking for hours, we almost gave up when we saw a small path. It was direct to the parking lot! We ran and found our car, happy to be safe.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Jake and his brother go hiking?
Correct: Their parents gave them a gift.
Distractors: They wanted to explore the forest. / They were training for a competition. / They were looking for a lost item.
Explanation: The text says, "Last summer, my brother and I went hiking in the forest as a gift from our parents." The correct answer is "Their parents gave them a gift." The other options are not mentioned in the text. "Exploring the forest" is not the reason—they went because it was a gift. "Training for a competition" and "looking for a lost item" are not supported by the text.
---
Q2: What made Jake’s brother nervous?
Correct: They couldn’t find the trails in the thick forest.
Distractors: They ran out of water. / They saw a wild animal. / They argued about the map.
Explanation: The text says, "The trees were too thick, and we couldn’t find the network of trails. My brother was nervous." The correct answer is "They couldn’t find the trails in the thick forest." The other options are not mentioned. They had water, no wild animal was seen, and they did not argue.
---
Q3: How did Jake and his brother finally find their way out?
Correct: They followed a small path that led directly to the parking lot.
Distractors: They called for help on their phones. / They waited for someone to rescue them. / They followed animal tracks.
Explanation: The text says, "After walking for hours, we almost gave up when we saw a small path. It was direct to the parking lot!" The correct answer is "They followed a small path that led directly to the parking lot." The other options are not mentioned. They did not use phones, wait for rescue, or follow animal tracks.



Topic: an adventure story about finding a secret door in the house

Text:
By Mark White

Last summer, I moved into an old house with my family. My father is a businessman, and he loves creative ideas. One day, while reading a novel in my room, I noticed something strange—a small, hidden panel behind my bookshelf. I pushed it, and a secret door opened!

I was lucky because no one else in my family had found it. Inside, there were old drawings, a cartoon of a pirate, and even a diary with evidence of another child who lived there years ago. The diary said, "I don’t like my new school. My relationship with my friends is weak, but this door makes me happy."

I didn’t recognize the handwriting, but the words made me think of myself. Now, I visit the secret room every day to draw my own adventures.

Questions:
Q1: What did Mark find behind the bookshelf?
Correct: A hidden door.
Distractors: A locked treasure chest. / A broken mirror. / A family photo album.
Explanation: The text says, "I noticed something strange—a small, hidden panel behind my bookshelf. I pushed it, and a secret door opened!" The correct answer is "A hidden door." The other options are not mentioned in the text and contradict the details given.
---
Q2: What was inside the secret room?
Correct: Old drawings, a cartoon, and a diary.
Distractors: A map and a sword. / A collection of coins. / A broken clock.
Explanation: The text describes "old drawings, a cartoon of a pirate, and even a diary." The correct answer matches these details. The other options are not supported by the text and are unrelated to what was found.
---
Q3: Why did Mark think the diary’s words made him think of himself?
Correct: The writer of the diary also felt lonely at school.
Distractors: The diary was written by his father. / The diary mentioned a secret door in his school. / The diary talked about a creative hobby.
Explanation: The text says, "The diary said, 'I don’t like my new school. My relationship with my friends is weak...'" This shows the writer felt lonely, similar to how Mark might feel. The other options are incorrect because the diary was not written by his father, did not mention a school door, and did not focus on hobbies.



Topic: an adventure story about finding a treasure

Text:
By Liam Carter

One sunny morning, Jake found an old map in his grandfather’s pocket. It showed a hidden treasure near the column in the town square. The map was round and had strange symbols. Jake’s friend Mia said, “This looks like an advertising poster, not a treasure map!” But Jake was sure it was real.

They followed the map to the square. The final clue was a wave pattern near the base of the column. Jake dug carefully and found a small box. Inside was a key and a note: “For the true possession of the king’s gold.” Mia suggested they look for the gold in the old castle. Jake smiled—this was the start of their greatest adventure!

Questions:
Q1: What did Jake find in his grandfather’s pocket?
Correct: An old map with strange symbols.
Distractors: A key to a treasure chest. / A note about a king’s gold. / A picture of an advertising poster.
Explanation: The text says, “Jake found an old map in his grandfather’s pocket. It showed a hidden treasure... The map was round and had strange symbols.” The correct answer matches this. “A key to a treasure chest” is wrong because the key was found later. “A note about a king’s gold” is incorrect because the note was inside the box, not in the pocket. “A picture of an advertising poster” is false because Mia thought it looked like an advertising poster, but it was actually a map.
---
Q2: What was the final clue to finding the treasure?
Correct: A wave pattern near the base of the column.
Distractors: A symbol on the advertising poster. / A note hidden in the castle. / A round stone in the town square.
Explanation: The text states, “The final clue was a wave pattern near the base of the column.” The correct answer directly matches this. “A symbol on the advertising poster” is wrong because the poster was just Mia’s mistaken idea. “A note hidden in the castle” is incorrect because the note was found after digging near the column. “A round stone in the town square” is false because the map led them to the column, not a random stone.
---
Q3: What did Mia suggest they do after finding the box?
Correct: Look for the gold in the old castle.
Distractors: Sell the key to a collector. / Ask the king for help. / Hide the treasure in the pocket.
Explanation: The text says, “Mia suggested they look for the gold in the old castle.” The correct answer matches this. “Sell the key to a collector” is wrong because the text does not mention selling anything. “Ask the king for help” is false because the king is only referenced in the note, not as a living person to ask. “Hide the treasure in the pocket” is incorrect because the treasure was just found, and they planned to search further.



Topic: a story about a day when everything went wrong but ended well

Text:
By Mark White

It was a cold autumn day, and Jake was in a hurry. He was an active soldier on leave, and he needed to finish his task before the train left. He ran to the station, but his illness made him weak. Suddenly, a car hit him! He fell and hurt his leg.

Luckily, a kind soldier at the station helped him. The soldier took Jake to a bar near the station. The owner, a lovely woman, called an ambulance. While waiting, Jake drank tea and felt better.

The train left without him, but Jake was happy. He had a new friend and a warm place to stay. His speed didn’t matter—what mattered was that he was safe.

Questions:
Q1: Why was Jake running to the station?
Correct: He was trying to catch a train before it left.
Distractors: He was late for work. / He was running to meet a friend. / He was exercising because he was sick.
Explanation: The text says, "He was an active soldier on leave, and he needed to finish his task before the train left." The correct answer is "He was trying to catch a train before it left." The other options are wrong because the text does not mention work, meeting a friend, or exercising due to illness.
---
Q2: What happened when Jake fell at the station?
Correct: A soldier helped him and took him to a nearby bar.
Distractors: A doctor treated him immediately. / He was taken to the hospital by a taxi. / He walked home alone.
Explanation: The text states, "Luckily, a kind soldier at the station helped him. The soldier took Jake to a bar near the station." The correct answer is "A soldier helped him and took him to a nearby bar." The other options are incorrect because the text does not mention a doctor, a taxi, or Jake walking home alone.
---
Q3: How did Jake feel at the end of the day?
Correct: Happy because he was safe and had made a new friend.
Distractors: Sad because he missed his train. / Angry because he hurt his leg. / Excited because he was going home.
Explanation: The text says, "He had a new friend and a warm place to stay. His speed didn’t matter—what mattered was that he was safe." The correct answer is "Happy because he was safe and had made a new friend." The other options are wrong because the text does not say Jake was sad, angry, or excited about going home.



Topic: a story about a time I helped a neighbor

Text:
By Mark Johnson

Recently, I helped my neighbor, Mr. Lee, a busy businessman. He was trying to figure out how to fix his old door, which was very thick and heavy. I offered my help, and he accepted my suggestion to use a new hinge. After working together, we fixed it quickly.

Later, Mr. Lee received an invitation to a party and asked me to go with him. I was happy to act as his guest. He was very grateful and said my help was a big factor in making his day better. He even gave me a small gift as a thank-you!

Questions:
Q1: Why did Mr. Lee ask Mark to help him?
Correct: Because Mark offered to fix his door.
Distractors: Because Mr. Lee was too tired to work alone. / Because Mark was the only person available. / Because Mr. Lee needed someone to carry the heavy door.
Explanation: The text says, "I offered my help, and he accepted my suggestion." This means Mark volunteered, not that Mr. Lee was too tired or that Mark was the only option. The door was heavy, but the text does not say Mark carried it—only that they fixed it together.
---
Q2: What did Mr. Lee give Mark as a thank-you?
Correct: A small gift.
Distractors: A higher rating for his work. / An extra invitation to another event. / A promise to help Mark in return.
Explanation: The text states, "He even gave me a small gift as a thank-you!" The other options are not mentioned. "Rating" is unrelated, and while Mr. Lee invited Mark to a party, this was not a gift. There is no mention of a promise to help Mark later.
---
Q3: What was one reason Mr. Lee was grateful to Mark?
Correct: Because Mark helped him fix the door quickly.
Distractors: Because Mark had a better rate of fixing things. / Because Mark was the only neighbor who offered help. / Because Mark was also a businessman.
Explanation: The text explains, "He was very grateful and said my help was a big factor in making his day better." The door was fixed quickly, which matches the correct answer. "Rate" is not mentioned, and while Mark was the one who helped, the text does not say he was the only neighbor who offered. Mr. Lee was a businessman, but Mark’s profession is not stated.



Topic: a story about my favorite toy from childhood

Text:
My Favorite Toy

When I was a child, my favorite toy was a small train set. It was incredible—the train moved on a track, and I could be the passenger! My father helped me build it. He said, "This is a fun process!" I loved playing with it every day.

One day, I was playing outside when I fall down. My knife (a toy one) cut my hand a little. I cried, but my mother said, "Ah, don’t worry!" She cleaned my hand immediately.

I wanted to publish a book about my train one day. Maybe I’ll do it when I grow up!

Questions:
Q1: What did the child love about the train set?
Correct: It was fun to play with and move on tracks.
Distractors: It was a real train with a real passenger. / It was a book about trains. / It was a knife for cutting things.
Explanation: The text says, "I loved playing with it every day" and "the train moved on a track." The correct answer is about playing and moving. The other options are wrong because the train was a toy, not real, and it was not a book or a knife.
---
Q2: What happened when the child fell down?
Correct: A toy knife cut their hand a little.
Distractors: They broke their arm. / They hurt their leg. / They fell on the train track.
Explanation: The text says, "My knife (a toy one) cut my hand a little." The correct answer matches this detail. The other options are wrong because the text does not mention a broken arm, a hurt leg, or falling on the track.
---
Q3: What did the child want to do with their train set when they grew up?
Correct: Write a book about it.
Distractors: Sell it to other children. / Give it to their father. / Keep playing with it forever.
Explanation: The text says, "I wanted to publish a book about my train one day." The correct answer is about writing a book. The other options are wrong because the text does not mention selling, giving, or keeping the train forever.



Topic: a story about my first day at school

Text:
By Mark Johnson

My first day at school was exciting but also a little scary. My parents were divorced, and I lived with my mum now. She gave me a new diary to write about my day. When I arrived, I saw a big playground with a round slide. I was nervous, but my mum told me to be brave.

At break time, I tried to hide behind a tree because I didn’t know anyone. Then, my coach from summer camp walked by. He smiled and said, "Ready for an experiment?" He helped me join a game of tag. After school, I washed my hands and wrote in my diary: "Today, I made a choice to be friendly!"

Questions:

Correct: To write about his experiences at school.
Distractors: To keep track of his homework. / To help him remember his schedule. / To practice writing letters.

---
Q2: What did Mark do at break time?
Correct: He played tag with his coach.
Distractors: He sat alone under a tree. / He joined a group of new friends. / He went to the slide.
Explanation: The text says, "My coach... helped me join a game of tag." The correct answer matches this. The distractors are wrong because Mark did not sit alone (he hid but then joined the game), did not join a group of new friends (only his coach helped him), and did not go to the slide (he saw it but did not play on it).

Topic: a story about a best friend I met when I was little

Text:
When I was five, my parents took me to a big department store to buy new shoes. On the second stair, I saw a boy my age sitting alone. He was printing a small picture with a toy camera. I asked if I could see it, and he smiled. His name was Leo, and he was very clever—he could fix anything with just a few tools.

According to his mother, Leo wanted to be an engineer when he grew up. I didn’t know what that meant, but it sounded exciting! We played together all day, and when we left, his mother gave me some cash for a snack. That was the first time I met my best friend.

We had many chances to play after that. Leo always seemed to know how to win at race games, but he never bragged. Now, I still remember how lucky I was to meet him.

Questions:
Q1: What did the boy Leo do in the department store?
Correct: He was printing a picture with a toy camera.
Distractors: He was selling small toys. / He was waiting for his parents. / He was fixing a broken toy.
Explanation: The text says, "I saw a boy my age sitting alone. He was printing a small picture with a toy camera." The correct answer matches this exactly. "He was selling small toys" is wrong because the text does not mention selling. "He was waiting for his parents" is false—his mother was with him, but he was not just waiting. "He was fixing a broken toy" is incorrect because the text describes him printing, not fixing.
---
Q2: Why did Leo’s mother give the narrator some cash?
Correct: To buy a snack.
Distractors: To pay for a toy. / To help with a game. / To share with Leo.
Explanation: The text states, "His mother gave me some cash for a snack." The correct answer directly matches this. "To pay for a toy" is wrong because the cash was for food, not a purchase. "To help with a game" is false—there is no mention of games involving money. "To share with Leo" is incorrect because the narrator received the cash, not Leo.
---
Q3: What did Leo want to be when he grew up?
Correct: An engineer.
Distractors: A photographer. / A toy seller. / A race driver.
Explanation: The text says, "According to his mother, Leo wanted to be an engineer when he grew up." The correct answer is "An engineer." "A photographer" is wrong because Leo was printing pictures, but the text does not say he wanted to be one. "A toy seller" is false—there is no mention of selling toys as a future job. "A race driver" is incorrect because the text only mentions Leo winning race games, not his career aspirations.

Topic: a story about helping a lost pet find its home

Text:
By Mark Thompson

Last summer, my neighbor’s dog, Max, went missing. Max was a golden retriever, and his owner, a nice guy named Tom, was very worried. Tom asked me for help because I had experience with dog training.

I suggested we put up posters including a photo of Max and his name. We also left a small quantity of his favorite treats near the door, hoping he would follow the smell. To our incredible surprise, Max returned the next day! Tom was so happy that he even offered to replace my old carpet, which had a stain from a leak.

Helping Max was a fun comedy of errors, but the real benefit was seeing Tom smile again. Now, Max sleeps on a new, clean carpet, and Tom promises to give him more exercise!

The other options are incorrect because the text does not mention teaching tricks, calling shelters, buying a collar, or taking Max to a vet.

Q2: Why was Tom happy after Max returned?
Correct: He was relieved to have his dog back.
Distractors: He won a prize for finding Max. / He got a new dog as a reward. / He fixed his leaky roof. / He replaced Mark’s old carpet.
Explanation: The text states, "Tom was so happy that he even offered to replace my old carpet," but the main reason for his happiness was "seeing Tom smile again" after Max returned. The correct answer is "He was relieved to have his dog back." Topic: a story about the first time I used a video call to talk to a family member far away

Text:
By Maria Lopez

Last summer, my grandfather moved to another city for work. It was hard because we lived far apart, and I missed seeing him. My parents said, "We need to find a helpful instrument to stay united." They taught me how to use video calls.

The first time I called him, I was nervous. I thought there may be some danger—what if the call didn’t work? But my grandfather smiled and said, "It’s like we’re in the same room!" We talked for an hour, and it felt like peace in our general life. Now, video calls suit us perfectly. They help families stay close, even when society keeps us apart.

Questions:

The festival has many traditions. Children carry lanterns in different shapes and colors, and some families perform dragon dances to avoid bad luck. The festival also has a variety of games, like guessing riddles about the moon. In some villages, people even try to balance a weight on their heads while walking—it’s a fun challenge!

Before the festival, my family does research to learn more about its history. My grandmother says the festival started as a way to thank the harvest. She also tells us that in old stories, a female goddess once helped people during a drought. Now, we celebrate her kindness too.

Topic: a fairy tale about a clever princess who solves puzzles to save her kingdom

Text:
Princess Elara was a teenage girl with a sharp mind. When a wicked king demanded three impossible puzzles to save her kingdom, she noticed a small diary in her pocket—her father’s old notes. Inside, he wrote a method to solve riddles by looking at the position of stars.

The first puzzle asked her to walk a distance no one could measure. Elara rated the moon’s glow and guessed the correct steps. The second tested her strength, but she was weak in arms, so she used a tip from the diary: "Think, don’t fight." The third puzzle trapped her in a maze, but she followed the stars’ path.

The king, amazed, let her rule. Elara kept the diary as proof of her cleverness.

Q1: What did Princess Elara find in her pocket that helped her solve the puzzles?
Correct: Her father’s old diary with riddle-solving notes.
Distractors: A magic wand. / A map of the kingdom. / A book about stars.

Correct: To measure the correct walking distance.
Distractors: To scare the wicked king. / To find her way in the maze. / To prove she was brave.
Explanation: The text explains, "Elara rated the moon’s glow and guessed the correct steps" for the first puzzle about distance. The other options are unrelated—she didn’t use the moon for fear, the maze, or bravery.

Distractors: Memory. / Speed. / Confidence.
Explanation: The text states, "she was weak in arms," and the diary’s tip was to "Think, don’t fight." This shows her weakness was physical, not mental or emotional. The other options (memory, speed, confidence) are not supported by the text.



Topic: a fairy tale about tiny people

Text:
By Sir Thistlewick

One sunny morning, Sir Thistlewick found a tiny diary in the wood near his castle. He was surprised because the handwriting was so small! Inside, he read about a group of tiny people who lived in a hollow tree. Their goal was to build a bridge over a small river.

Sir Thistlewick decided to apply their help. "I need a dry place to store my books," he said. The tiny people agreed. They worked hard for a week, using their finger to carry tiny stones. But when they finished, Sir Thistlewick’s cat burn the bridge by accident!

The tiny people were sad, but they had a secondly plan. They built a new bridge underground. Sir Thistlewick was impressed and gave them a golden key as a reward.

By Emma White

The villagers were afraid of him because he burned their houses and smelled terrible. One day, a brave pilot named Leo arrived for a tour of the mountains. He wanted to communicate with Smolder to help him change.

Leo borrowed an old book with a code to talk to dragons. He read it aloud near Smolder’s cave. The dragon listened carefully and realized he was lonely. He shut his eyes and promised to stop being evil. Now, Smolder helps villagers and even teaches children to be independent.

Topic: a story about a school sports day

Text:
By Jake Carter

Last week, our school had a big sports day. The sun was shining, and everyone was excited. I wanted to lead the race, but I had a terrible headache all morning. My friend Sam said, "Don’t worry! Just take this drug—it will help!" But I knew it was rubbish—it was just his old cough medicine. I drank water instead.

During the race, I almost fell when a strong blow of wind hit me. But I passed the finish line in second place! After the race, we sat on old furniture under a tree. Someone dropped a book near my feet, and I saw a little blood on the page—probably from a cut. The teacher said, "That’s strange! It looks like someone was reading underground!" We all laughed.

Questions:

Correct: He knew it was useless.
Distractors: He thought it would make him faster. / He was afraid it was dangerous. / He drank it because he trusted Sam.

- "He thought it would make him faster" is false—Jake didn’t believe it would help.
- "He was afraid it was dangerous" is not mentioned; Jake just called it rubbish.
- "He drank it because he trusted Sam" is wrong—he drank water instead.
---
Q2: What happened during Jake’s race?
Correct: A strong wind almost made him fall.
Distractors: He tripped over a book. / He won the race easily. / He got hurt and had to stop.
Explanation: The text states, "During the race, I almost fell when a strong blow of wind hit me." This matches the correct answer. The other options are wrong because:
- "He tripped over a book" is false—the book was dropped after the race.
- "He won the race easily" is incorrect; he finished second.
- "He got hurt and had to stop" is not true—he completed the race.
---
Q3: Why did the teacher say, "That’s strange! It looks like someone was reading underground!"?
Correct: There was blood on the book’s page.
Distractors: The book was buried in the ground. / The teacher thought someone hid the book. / The book had a secret message.
Explanation: The text explains, "I saw a little blood on the page—probably from a cut." The teacher’s joke implies the blood made it look like the book was read "underground" (like a secret or hidden place). The other options are wrong because:
- "The book was buried in the ground" is not mentioned.
- "The teacher thought someone hid the book" is not supported by the text.
- "The book had a secret message" is not stated—only the blood was unusual.

Topic: a story about a time I was dressed completely wrong for the weather

Text:

Last winter, I went to a big city for a job interview. I was very excited but forgot to check the weather. The guide at the hotel told me it was going to be cold, but I didn’t listen. I wore shorts and a T-shirt—necessary clothes for summer, not winter!

When I left the hotel, the wind was strong, and snowflakes started to fall. I recognized my mistake immediately. My hands were freezing, so I held my arms tightly. I tried to walk slowly to an electronic store to buy warm clothes. The shop provided me with a thick coat and gloves, and I finished my shopping quickly.

Now, I always check the weather before I travel. It’s better to be prepared than to invent excuses for being cold!

Topic: a fairy tale about talking animals who helped each other

Text:
By Lily Green

On a sunny morning, a clever fox named Finn lived on a quiet farm near a sparkling lake. One day, he found a brilliant old book with pictures of talking animals. "This is ideal!" he thought. That night, he read a spell aloud and—poof!—the animals came to life!

First, a kind rabbit named Bella hopped over. "I’ve been waiting for this opportunity!" she said. Soon, a wise old owl named Oliver flew down. Together, they decided to help the farmer, who was sad because his crops were failing.

Finn had a figure in his head: they would work along the fields, scaring away pests. Bella dug small holes, Oliver watched from above, and Finn used his quick paws to print seeds in the right places. The farmer was amazed! "You’re definitely the best helpers I’ve ever had!" he cheered.

At night, they met at a cozy bar by the lake to share stories. The animals laughed, knowing they had made the farm—and each other’s lives—happier.

Topic: a fairy tale about a friendly giant who was afraid of mice

Text:

Bruno’s teenage twin brothers, Max and Leo, laughed at him. "You’re afraid of a mouse?" they teased. Bruno felt embarrassed. That night, he heard a loud noise—a lorry had broken down near the bridge. The driver needed help, but Bruno hesitated. "What if there are mice in the lorry?" he whispered. His brothers rolled their eyes but followed him anyway.

As they fixed the lorry, Bruno saw a mouse peeking out. His heart pounded, but he remembered the driver’s scared face. Taking a deep breath, he gently moved the mouse away. The driver, who was the village president, was so grateful that he declared Bruno the bravest giant in the land. From that day on, Bruno still feared mice—but he was no longer afraid to help others.

One evening, Benny chatted with his sister, Lily. "Do you think I can marry the wind?" he asked. Lily smiled. "Just focus on flying first!"

Finally, Benny entered the sky race. He jumped, flapped his ears, and—whoosh!—he flew! The villagers cheered. "You’re the winner!" they shouted. Benny smiled. "I did it! But remember, hard work matters more than dreams."

Topic: a fairy tale about a mouse who lived in a library and learned to read

Text:
By Emma White

In a big, old library, there lived a small mouse named Milo whose name meant "brave." Milo loved books but couldn’t read. Every night, he watched the librarian flying (on her broomstick!) as she arranged books. One day, Milo found a dry, dusty book about mice. He tried to read it, but the words looked like rubbish!

Milo’s friend, a crazy owl, said, "You need a system! Read one page every day." Milo followed the plan. Soon, he knew the exact words on the first page. But then—disaster! A virus (from a sneeze!) made the books’ ink run. Milo had to pay attention to fix them.

Now, Milo reads every night. He’s the library’s helper, and no one calls him the worst mouse anymore!

Topic: a story about a person who could guess exactly what time it was without a clock

Text:
By Mark Thompson

When I was a child, I lived in a small flat with my grandparents. They never had a clock, but I could always figure out the time by listening to the sounds outside. My grandfather said it was a strange skill, but I never understood its purpose—until I grew up.

One day, I referred to an old book and learned that my ability might be linked to how my brain developed. Scientists call it "chronesthesia," the skill to sense time without an instrument. My grandfather used to say, "Time is like a sock—you can’t see it, but you feel it." Now, I study this at university, but I still don’t earn cash from it. My lecture on the topic was funny—students laughed when I said I once guessed the time by a bird’s song!

Questions:
Q1: What did Mark’s grandfather say about time?
Correct: Time is like a sock—you can’t see it, but you feel it.
Distractors: Time is like a code you can learn. / Time is an instrument you must use. / Time is something only scientists can figure out.
Explanation:
The text directly quotes Mark’s grandfather: "Time is like a sock—you can’t see it, but you feel it." The correct answer matches this exactly. The other options are incorrect because:
- "Time is like a code you can learn" is false—nowhere does the text mention time as a code.
- "Time is an instrument you must use" is wrong because the text says Mark’s brain worked like an instrument (for sensing time), not that time itself is an instrument.

As they descended, they heard a strange sound—a deep, rhythmic drum echoing from the cliffs. Jake’s heart raced. Was it an animal? Or something worse? His mind reminded him of old stories about the mountain’s enemy—a lost climber who had vanished decades ago.

At the base, Jake framed the experience in his mind: a lesson in listening to warnings. “Next time,” he joked, “I’ll bring a map—and a louder voice.”

Topic: a story about an unexpected act of kindness

Text:

The woman’s face reflected gratitude as she sipped her tea. Later, I learned she was a retired schoolteacher who had spent her life teaching children to read. Her excitement at sharing stories about her students made me realize how small acts of kindness could change someone’s day. The café owner later told me he often did this for regulars—it was the basis of their luxury service: kindness over profit.

Even the café’s printing on the menus—elegant but simple—seemed to reflect their philosophy: beauty in simplicity. The following week, I returned with a book of short stories, hoping to brighten her day again.

When I was 12, I visited a western village in Spain for the first time. The attraction of the ancient castle made me mad with curiosity. For years, I researched festivals, practiced public speaking, and even took a mild course in event management. Finally, at 25, I convinced the village council to let me plan a small event.

The first festival was a disaster—rain ruined the outdoor performances, and half the guests left early. But I didn’t go home. Instead, I learned from my mistakes. The next year, I prepared for everything: tents, backup music, and even a mild heating system for chilly evenings. By the third festival, the village was packed. Now, my festival is famous, and I’m still hosting it every summer.

Topic: a fairy tale about a woman who talks to birds and they help her

Text:

One day, a poisonous fog rolled in, killing the crops and leaving the villagers in despair. The mayor, desperate to save the harvest, ordered the use of chemical sprays, but Elara refused, fearing harm to the land.

“Let me try,” she said, though no one believed her. She sat by the river, humming softly, and soon, a flock of sparrows landed near her. They whispered of a hidden spring where clean water flowed. Elara followed their guidance, and when she reached the spring, her breathing slowed as she drank the pure liquid. The birds chirped excitedly, as if convincing her of its power.

She returned to the village and asked the villagers to gather at dawn. As the sun rose, she sprinkled the water over the fields. Slowly, the fog lifted, and the crops revived. The villagers were amazed. Though some still doubted her, they could no longer ignore the mention of the birds’ help. From then on, Elara’s mood brightened, and the village thrived, thanks to her unique bond with nature.

Distractors: They were afraid of the poisonous fog. / They thought the chemical sprays were safer. / They didn’t trust her relation with birds.
Explanation:
The text states, “though no one believed her,” meaning the villagers doubted her method. The fog was dangerous, but that wasn’t the reason for refusal (it was already killing crops). The chemical sprays were the mayor’s failed solution, not the villagers’ preference. While some may have distrusted her bond with birds, the text emphasizes disbelief in her approach, not outright rejection of her relation with nature.

Distractors: They sang to scare away the poisonous fog. / They carried the water to the fields themselves. / They convinced the villagers to trust her immediately.
Explanation:
The text says, “a flock of sparrows landed near her. They whispered of a hidden spring,” confirming they led her to the water. The fog wasn’t scared away by singing, and the birds didn’t physically carry water or instantly convince the villagers—the villagers only changed their minds after seeing the results.

Correct: Using chemical sprays.
Distractors: Building a wall to block the fog. / Asking Elara for help immediately. / Moving the village away from the port.
Explanation:
The text states, “the mayor ordered the use of chemical sprays,” which failed. There’s no mention of walls, immediate help from Elara, or relocating the village. The port’s role is only mentioned as the village’s location, not as a solution.

One evening, Liam found the stone again. This time, he wished for his family to always have enough money. The stone glowed brighter than ever. But when he woke up, his father’s wallet was still empty. Had the wish failed? Or was the stone only for small pleasures, not big dreams?

Liam put the stone back in the attic, unsure if he’d ever use it again. Maybe some magic wasn’t meant to count for much in the end.

Liam had been its leader, using the library as a contact point for those in poverty.

Clara realized Liam wasn’t a threat—he was a guardian. The town decided to restore the library, turning it into a museum. In gratitude, Liam’s spirit finally rested, his charge fulfilled. The library became a place of honor, and Clara even received an award for her discovery.

My grandfather had told me stories about the statue’s past, and suddenly, I felt a strange connection. I tied a red rope around it, as if to protect it—and my creativity.

Years later, I became an educated artist, and that little statue now sits in my studio. The small step of cleaning it led to a life-changing development in my career.

Even a stadium-sized archive in Tokyo now holds recordings of endangered tongues, proving how seriously the world is taking this issue.

This letter is just a reference for the future. Treat it like a map, not a rulebook. You’ll make your own path, but I hope these words guide you when you need them.

Love,

His determination inspired me. When I was embarrassed by my first writing attempts, he told me, "Don’t let fear push you away from your dreams." Later, I joined a writing program at university, just like he had. His shift from translation to storytelling taught me that passion can change everything.

One day, I saw a video of a helicopter pilot explaining how to fasten seatbelts. The words were simple, but the context was exciting. I learned useful phrases like "Check the treatment of the ropes" (meaning "Make sure the ropes are secure"). Now, I feel absolutely confident in conversations.

Topic: a story about an unusual person I met while travelling

Text:

Last summer, I travelled to a small island in the Mediterranean where I met a man who changed my perspective on life. His name was Luca, and he was unlike anyone I’d ever known. He believed the ocean gave back only to those who respected its flow.

Luca regularly held a small ceremony before setting out: he would drop a coin into the water—a vote for the sea’s protection. Locals thought he was mad, but I admired his dedication. One evening, I watched as his boat sank slightly in rough waves. Instead of panicking, he laughed and said, “The sea tests us, but it never makes a victim of those who listen.” He personally taught me how to mend a fishing net, and I still stick to the habit of thanking the water before swimming.

What stayed with me most was his wisdom: “Life is like the tide—sometimes it pulls you under, but if you breathe with it, you rise again.”

Topic: a story about a time capsule

Text:
Last summer, my grandfather decided to bury a time capsule in our backyard. He had worked in a commercial printing company for 40 years and was ready to retire. The idea came to him during a family battle over where to store old photos—someone suggested throwing them away, but he refused.

Instead, he gathered basic items: a newspaper from 2023, a USB drive with family videos, and even a toy car from my childhood. We dug a hole near the old oak tree, and he personally placed the capsule inside. "Let the next generation pull it out when I’m gone," he said with a smile.

My uncle, who works in management, thought it was a waste of space, but my grandfather insisted it was convenient—no need for a bank vault. The local court even gave us a certificate to prove the burial was legal. Now, we wait for the day when we can open it together.

At first, people laughed. "It’s silly!" one member said. But soon, they began to appreciate the challenge. "It’s not just about enjoying the story," Mark explained. "It’s about understanding why the author wrote it that way." Some suffered at first, struggling to follow the plot, but others found it sharpened their skills.

The club wasn’t just about books—it was about ourselves. "You realize how much you used to miss when reading normally," said Lisa, a regular. The group even started a proper tradition: after each meeting, they shared one honest opinion about the book’s ending.

One technique came from a religious group I joined briefly—they used prayer times to channel focus. At first, I doubted it would work, but setting short, sacred breaks actually reduced my difficulty in starting tasks. Later, I tried the "Pomodoro Net" method, where I worked for 25 minutes and rested for 5. It felt artificial at first, but soon became my rhythm.

The biggest lesson? Time management isn’t about rigid rules—it’s about finding what helps you live without burnout. Some days, I still use the tape method; others, I just listen to music and trust my instincts.

Interior design trends change quickly, but some recent ideas seem highly unusual. One headline-grabbing trend is "earthy luxury," where designers use rough materials like leather and soil to create a natural, organic feel. For example, a living room might feature a sofa made of reclaimed leather paired with walls covered in a mix of clay and soil. This style represents a return to simplicity, but it also requires careful planning to avoid looking messy.

Another surprising trend is "helicopter decor," where furniture is designed to look like it’s floating or suspended in mid-air. Chairs with invisible supports or tables that appear to hover are becoming popular in modern homes. Some designers even use quotation marks from famous artworks to create floating shelves, adding an artistic touch. Critics argue that this style is impractical, but fans say it assists in making spaces feel more open and dreamlike.

In comparison to traditional designs, these trends focus on bold statements rather than comfort. While some may not last, they show how creativity can transform even the most ordinary rooms.

The captain, a former rugby player, joked, “At least we didn’t freeze like last time!” But Elena knew this discovery was too important to ignore.

Topic: an adventure story about a person who survived on an uninhabited island

Text:
By Maria Chen

When I was 22, I found myself stranded on a small island in the eastern Pacific after my boat broke down. The location was remote, and the nearest village was weeks away. With no money, I had to survive using only what I could find. My family had always lived in poverty, so I knew how to make things last.

At first, I tried to signal for help, but the weather was unpredictable. Then I remembered my grandfather’s advice: "If you can’t change the storm, switch your focus." I started collecting rainwater and building a shelter from driftwood. I even found a property map hidden in my backpack—it showed a nearby island where I might find fresh water.

One day, I saw a group of fishermen. I waved, but they ignored me. Later, I realized they spoke a different dialect, so I participated in their fishing rituals to show goodwill. They were shocked by my similarity to their ancestors—we shared the same standard of hard work.

After three months, a rescue team arrived. They asked why I hadn’t tried to escape sooner. I laughed and said, "I was too busy learning how to live like a king—even if it was just sand and coconuts."

Topic: an article about the planets of the Solar System

Text:
Our Solar System is a fascinating place, full of planets that each have their own unique features. While Earth is our home, other planets like Venus shine so brightly in the sky that ancient civilizations believed it was a competitor to the Sun. Venus’s thick atmosphere traps heat, making it the hottest planet—even hotter than Mercury, which is closest to the Sun. This extreme climate makes Venus a tight spot for any future explorers.

Mars, often called the "Red Planet," has long been a focus for scientists. Its surface is covered in rusty dust, and some believe it once had water, making it a potential client for future colonization. However, the planet’s thin atmosphere means any visitors would need advanced technology to survive.

Jupiter, the largest planet, is a gas giant with no solid surface. Its communication with Earth is limited, but its massive storms, like the Great Red Spot, have been studied for centuries. Meanwhile, Saturn’s stunning rings make it a favorite among photographers and space enthusiasts.

Smaller planets like Uranus and Neptune are icy and distant, but they still play a role in our Solar System’s balance. Even Pluto, though now classified as a dwarf planet, remains a subject of curiosity. Exploring these worlds helps us understand our place in the universe—and reminds us how grateful we should be for Earth’s perfect conditions. After all, no other planet has the right mix of air, water, and legal protections for life as we know it.

Topic: an article about the unusual New Year traditions in different cultures

Text:
New Year’s Eve is a time for celebration, but some traditions around the world are far from familiar. In Denmark, people smash plates against their neighbors’ doors to bring good luck—a practice that might seem strange to those who experienced it for the first time! The noise is believed to bury bad luck from the past year.

In Spain, eating 12 grapes at midnight is a must. Each grape represents a month, and people afford no mistakes—they must finish them all before the clock strikes 12. Meanwhile, in Greece, families gather to knock on their front doors with a piece of bread wrapped in a cloth. This ritual, called xerosfago, is said to ensure prosperity for the coming year.

In Scotland, the Hogmanay festival includes a slow dance called the "Ceilidh," where people hold hands in a circle. Some traditions are quieter, like in Japan, where people visit temples to bury their regrets and start fresh. Others, like Brazil’s lonely "Queimada" ritual, involve burning an effigy to symbolize the end of the old year.

While some customs are based on ancient beliefs, others have evolved into modern celebrations. What unites them all is the hope for a better year ahead.

Questions:
Q1: What do Danish people do to bring good luck during New Year’s Eve?
Correct: They smash plates against their neighbors’ doors.
Distractors: They eat 12 grapes at midnight. / They knock on doors with bread wrapped in cloth. / They burn an effigy to symbolize the end of the old year.
Explanation: The text states that Danes "smash plates against their neighbors’ doors to bring good luck." Eating grapes is a Spanish tradition, knocking with bread is Greek, and burning an effigy is Brazilian. These are all plausible but incorrect based on the text.

Topic: an article about the principles of healthy eating

Text:
By Dr. Elena Carter

Eating healthily doesn’t have to be boring—it can even be exciting! Many people believe that healthy food is unpleasant or tasteless, but this is often a lie. The key is to focus on natural ingredients and avoid processed foods. For example, instead of white bread, try whole-grain cotton-seeded bread (yes, it’s a thing!) or quinoa. These lead to better digestion and more energy.

Some people try extreme experiments with diets, like cutting out entire food groups. This can backfire, causing fatigue or even horror-like cravings. The best approach is balance. My grandmother always said, “A place at the table for everything—and everything in its place.” She meant variety: fruits, vegetables, lean proteins, and healthy fats.

Success in healthy eating comes from small changes. Start with one meal a day—maybe breakfast—and build from there. Many people successfully adopt this version of eating by planning ahead. After all, even the best intentions can fail without preparation.

Topic: an adventure story about a round-the-world journey

Text:
By Emma Carter

When I was 22, I decided to travel the world for a year. I had saved enough money and wanted to prove that I could live on my own. My first stop was India, where I stayed with a family in a small village. They dressed me in traditional cloth and taught me how to exchange money at local markets. I was amazed by the colors and smells—there was plenty to see!

Next, I went to Brazil, where I joined a group of volunteers helping in the Amazon rainforest. One day, we saw a rare bird, and I shot a photo with my old camera. The locals were happy, but some protested when they thought I was hunting. I had to explain that I was just a traveler documenting my journey.

In Africa, I met an experienced guide who took me on a safari. We saw lions and elephants, and he taught me to respect wildlife. He also shared stories about his life, and I wrote them all in my travel account.

By the end of my trip, I had learned so much. I had faced risks, but I was glad I did it. Now, I tell everyone: "Travel changes you—just take the chance!"

Taking photos was only mentioned in Brazil, not India. Protesting traditional clothing is false—she embraced it. She didn’t exchange money for her whole trip, just locally.

Last summer, I joined a local photography club as a volunteer. One day, an elderly gentleman approached me at a market and asked if I could take his photo. I hesitated, but he was so honest about his admiration that I agreed to sell it for a small price.

A few weeks later, I received a check for a much larger sum than I’d expected. It turned out the photo had sold at an auction for thousands of dollars! I still can’t believe I missed the chance to recognize him earlier. Now, I divide my time between photography and helping others discover their hidden talents.

Topic: a personal story about how I successfully formed a good habit

One morning, a stranger stopped to admire my progress. "You’ve come a long way!" he said. His encouragement made me totally committed. Now, jogging isn’t just a habit—it’s part of my routine, and I can’t imagine skipping it.

Different methods work for different people, so try various techniques. The key is consistency. Even tiny steps—like taking deep breaths or laughing at a funny video—can reduce stress over time. I started by dividing my monthly budget into jars labeled "Rent," "Food," and "Fun." Instead of writing numbers, I filled each jar with coins representing my account balance. The tail end of the month meant no more coins left for extras. It worked generally better than anything else. Even now, I keep a small jar on my desk. The touch of real money makes me think twice before spending.

My outdoors-loving friends thought it was silly, but the method saved me from debt forever. I even opened a bank account just to track my coins properly. Now, I recommend it to everyone—especially those who hate math!

Topic: an article about polar days and nights

Text:
Polar days and nights are unique natural phenomena that occur near the Arctic and Antarctic circles. During the summer, the sun never sets in these regions, creating a period called the polar day, where viewers can enjoy 24 hours of daylight. Conversely, in winter, the sun remains below the horizon, resulting in the polar night, where darkness lasts for weeks. These extremes make life practical for locals, who must adapt to long periods indoors or outdoors.

Scientists use a diagram to explain how Earth’s tilt causes these events. The equal length of daylight and darkness at the equator contrasts sharply with the secondary effects near the poles. For example, in Svalbard, Norway, residents experience months without sunlight, affecting agriculture and wildlife. A polar bear’s ability to survive depends on its bite strength and fat reserves during the long winter.

Tourists often visit to witness these phenomena, but locals must judge the best times for outdoor activities. While the election of the best season for travel depends on personal preference, the profit from tourism helps local economies. Understanding these cycles is crucial for both survival and business in polar regions.

Questions:
Q1: What causes polar days and nights?
Correct: Earth’s tilt
Distractors: The moon’s gravity / A secondary solar eclipse / The Earth’s rotation speed
Explanation: The text explains that Earth’s tilt causes the sun to stay above or below the horizon for extended periods near the poles. The moon’s gravity affects tides, not daylight cycles. A secondary solar eclipse is unrelated, and while Earth’s rotation affects day/night cycles globally, it doesn’t explain the extended polar phenomena. The text explicitly mentions the tilt in relation to the diagram.
---
Q2: How do locals in Svalbard adapt to the polar night?
Correct: They rely on indoor activities and fat reserves.
Distractors: They migrate to warmer regions. / They use artificial sunlight year-round. / They depend on imported food only.
Explanation: The text states that locals must adapt to long periods indoors and mentions polar bears using fat reserves, implying humans face similar challenges. Migration isn’t mentioned, and while artificial light might help, the text doesn’t claim it’s universal. Imported food isn’t highlighted as the primary adaptation.

---

Distractors: Only scientists can live there comfortably. / Polar nights are shorter than polar days. / Outdoors activities are always safe.

Topic: an article about unusual alternative sources of energy

Text:
In many western countries, people rely on traditional energy sources like coal or oil. However, scientists are exploring unusual alternatives to reduce pollution and costs. One surprising option is piezoelectric energy, which comes from pressure. For example, whenever someone walks on a special floor, tiny crystals inside generate electricity. This medium-sized technology is already used in some entry-level smartphones to power small devices.

Another idea is human waste energy. In some cities, biogas plants convert sewage into electricity, but a slightly more advanced system uses urine to create hydrogen fuel. While this might sound strange, it’s a safe way to produce energy without harming the environment. Some people are annoyed by the idea, but experts say it could work at a large level.

Apart from these, wave energy is gaining attention. Devices placed in the ocean capture the power of waves and convert it into usable electricity. The technical challenges are high, but if successful, it could provide clean energy for coastal areas.

Today, we’re more aware of technology, but ancient methods still teach us patience and respect for nature.

Topic: an article about how the continents formed

Text:
The Earth’s continents were not always where they are today. Scientists treat the theory of plate tectonics as the best explanation for their movement. Millions of years ago, all landmasses were joined in a supercontinent called Pangaea. Over time, these massive plates drifted apart, shaping the world we know.

One remote clue comes from fossils of ancient plants, like leaf fossils found in South America and Africa. These plants could not have grown in such different climates if the continents had always been separate. Another impression comes from matching mountain ranges, such as the Appalachians in North America and the Caledonides in Europe.

Some people mistakenly think these changes happened quickly, but geological processes are slow. For example, dust from volcanic eruptions or even human activities can locate new layers of sediment, but true continental drift takes millions of years. Even a photographer capturing Earth from space would see only tiny shifts over a lifetime.

Interestingly, early humans might have treated the idea of moving continents as illegal nonsense—after all, the script of ancient myths often described a fixed world. Yet science proves otherwise.

Correct: Scientists consider it the best explanation for how continents moved.
Distractors: It was proven illegal by ancient myths. / It explains how dust forms new continents. / It was first discovered by a photographer.
Explanation: The text states, "Scientists treat the theory of plate tectonics as the best explanation for their movement." The other options are incorrect because the text mentions myths being treated as fixed (not illegal), dust locates sediment (not forms continents), and no photographer is linked to the theory.
---
Q2: How do leaf fossils provide evidence for continental drift?
Correct: They show plants existed in climates that are now separated by oceans.
Distractors: They prove continents moved faster than scientists thought. / They were used to write ancient scripts. / They were found only in remote deserts.
Explanation: The text explains, "fossils of ancient plants... found in South America and Africa" could not have grown in their current climates if the continents had always been separate. The other options contradict the text—speed of movement is not mentioned, fossils were not used for scripts, and their location is not limited to deserts.
---
Q3: Why might early humans have rejected the idea of moving continents?
Correct: Their myths described a fixed world.
Distractors: They thought it was an illegal scientific theory. / They relied on dust from volcanoes for proof. / They were influenced by a famous photographer’s work.
Explanation: The text says, "early humans might have treated the idea... as illegal nonsense—after all, the script of ancient myths often described a fixed world." The other options are incorrect because the text does not mention illegality as a scientific term, dust as proof, or a photographer’s influence.

Q4: What does the text imply about geological changes?
Correct: They happen over millions of years, not quickly.
Distractors: They are caused by human activities like photography. / They can be seen clearly in a single lifetime. / They are described in ancient scripts as fast.
Explanation: The text states, "geological processes are slow" and "true continental drift takes millions of years." The other options are wrong because human activities (like photography) are not causes, shifts are not visible in a lifetime, and ancient scripts describe a fixed world, not fast changes.



Topic: an article about inventions inspired by nature

Text:
Nature has always been a source of inspiration for human inventions. For example, the idea of Velcro came from a Swiss engineer named George de Mestral. While walking his dog in the woods, he noticed how burrs stuck to his pants and his pet’s fur. This romantic moment led him to study the tiny hooks on the burrs under a microscope. By 1948, he had created Velcro—a material that mimics nature’s design. Today, it’s used in everything from shoes to medical albums.

Another famous example is the design of the Shinkansen, Japan’s high-speed train. Engineers were inspired by the beak of the kingfisher bird, which splits water cleanly as it dives. By shaping the train’s nose like the bird’s beak, they reduced noise and improved efficiency. This contrast between nature and technology shows how animals can solve problems better than humans alone.

Not all inventions are as educated as these, but many follow the same principle. For instance, the lotus leaf’s self-cleaning surface inspired scientists to create water-repellent paints. Even simple ideas, like the pan used in solar cookers, come from observing how the sun’s rays heat objects. Some people argue that these innovations are just worse than human-made solutions, but authorities in engineering disagree. They say nature has been refining designs for millions of years—long before humans appeared.

One challenge is convincing people to trust nature’s solutions. Some industries prefer traditional methods, fearing change. However, as more inventions prove successful, skepticism fades. From Velcro to bullet trains, nature continues to teach us that the best ideas are often hiding in plain sight—till we look closely enough.

Questions:
Q1: What inspired George de Mestral to invent Velcro?
Correct: A walk in the woods where burrs stuck to his clothes.
Distractors: A study of animal fur under a microscope / A romantic idea from an old album / An authority in engineering suggesting the idea.
Explanation:
The text states that de Mestral noticed burrs sticking to his pants and his dog’s fur during a walk, leading to his invention. The microscope study came after this observation, not before. The album and authority are not mentioned as sources of inspiration.
---
Q2: How did the Shinkansen train’s design improve efficiency?
Correct: By mimicking the shape of a kingfisher’s beak.
Distractors: By using a pan to reflect sunlight / By studying the worse designs of older trains / By convincing engineers to use educated guesses.
Explanation:
The text explains that engineers shaped the train’s nose like a kingfisher’s beak to reduce noise and improve efficiency. The pan and worse designs are unrelated, and the idea was based on observation, not guesses.
---
Q3: Why do some industries resist nature-inspired inventions?
Correct: They fear change and prefer traditional methods.
Distractors: They believe nature’s solutions are worse than human-made ones / They lack the authority to approve new designs / They are educated only in old technologies.
Explanation:
The text mentions skepticism due to fear of change, not a belief that nature’s solutions are inferior. The authority and educated options are not supported by the text.
---
Q4: What does the text suggest about nature’s role in human innovation?
Correct: Nature provides refined solutions that humans can learn from.
Distractors: Nature’s ideas are only useful for romantic or artistic purposes / Humans have already solved all problems nature could teach us / The best inventions come from till humans ignore nature completely.
Explanation:
The text argues that nature’s designs, refined over millions of years, offer valuable lessons for human innovation. The other options contradict the text’s emphasis on learning from nature.



Topic: an article about unusual etiquette rules from around the world

In Japan, there is a unique etiquette rule that might surprise visitors: when eating noodles, it’s polite to slurp loudly. This isn’t just a tip—it’s a way to remind others that the meal is delicious and relaxing. Meanwhile, in South Korea, it’s considered rude to eat while walking, even on a busy campus. Instead, people stop to enjoy their food properly.

Another unusual rule comes from India, where some communities believe it’s bad luck to clap during a ceremony. Instead, they use a determined hand gesture to show approval. In contrast, in parts of Africa, guests at a wedding might be asked to walk backwards out of the venue to bring good fortune.

Even in academic settings, etiquette can be strange. In some universities, students are expected to bow slightly when greeting a professor, in addition to saying hello. Meanwhile, in a British court of law, witnesses must always face the judge, never turn their backs.

Explanation: The text explains that walking backwards is believed to bring good fortune, so the correct answer is "to bring good luck." The other options are not supported by the text.

Distractors: Wearing formal clothes to class. / Never asking questions in lectures. / Using a tip to pay for tuition.

Just as they turned to leave, the door slammed shut. The UV light flickered, revealing faint blue marks on the walls—proof the house was haunted after all. With one last deep breath, they escaped, vowing never to return.

Topic: a personal story about living in a lighthouse

Text:
By Emma Carter

When I was 18, I got a job as a keeper’s assistant in a lighthouse on the coast of Scotland. The lighthouse was an old type—built in the 1800s—with a spiral staircase and a silent engine room. My boss, a retired navy coach, taught me everything. The first night, I was nervous, but he said, “You’ll get used to it. The sea is naturally calming.”

The island was small, with only a few trees—mostly a twisted branch of pine—and a tiny village. My duties included polishing the brass pan that held the lamp and checking the latest weather reports. The authority that ran the lighthouses warned us never to cheat on safety checks, even if the storm outside was terrifying.

One evening, a ship signaled for help. I ran to the radio room and sent a message, but the storm made communication difficult. By dawn, the coast guard arrived, and the crew was safe. That moment made me realize how important my job was.

Liam barely had time to process her words before a group of armed guards spotted him. The woman handed him a small device. “Use this to pick a safe moment to return,” she instructed. As Liam scrambled to understand, she added, “The proposal for your disappearance is already in the system—just don’t let them print your face on every screen.” With a flick of her wrist, she vanished, leaving Liam to figure out how to survive in a world that had no place for him.

Topic: a story about a cross-cultural misunderstanding

Text:
When Mark, a young American businessman, arrived in Japan for his first business trip, he was eager to make a good impression. His company had sent him to negotiate a partnership with a traditional Japanese firm, and he had spent weeks preparing. However, his first meeting with the executives went poorly.

Mark, accustomed to direct communication, began the discussion by immediately presenting his company’s ideas. He assumed a neutral tone, but the Japanese executives remained silent, exchanging only brief, polite smiles. After a few awkward minutes, Mark realized he had made a mistake. In Japanese culture, directness can be seen as rude, and business discussions often begin with small talk and indirect hints. His attempt to impress them with confidence had instead caused trouble.

Later, Mark learned that his ancestors had once traded with Japan, and he wondered if this historical connection could help. He decided to research Japanese customs and accommodate their communication style. At his next meeting, he started with broad compliments about the company’s reputation and asked about their views on globalization before gently introducing his ideas. This time, the executives engaged warmly, and the negotiations progressed smoothly.

Afterwards, Mark reflected on the experience. He had assumed that his power as a representative of a multinational company would be enough to secure respect, but he soon understood that cultural sensitivity was far more important. The lesson stuck with him, and he made sure to adapt his approach in future meetings.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Mark’s first meeting with the Japanese executives go poorly?
Correct: He misunderstood their communication style.
Distractors: He was unprepared for the negotiation / He spoke too loudly / He refused to compromise on his ideas
Explanation: The text states that Mark assumed a neutral tone and presented his ideas directly, which the Japanese executives found inappropriate. His mistake was cultural, not a lack of preparation, volume, or refusal to compromise. The executives’ silence was due to his directness, not his unwillingness to negotiate.

Correct: He overestimated the effectiveness of directness.
Distractors: He tried to intimidate them / He was overly humble / He relied on his company’s reputation
Explanation: The phrase implies Mark believed confidence and directness would work, but his trouble arose because this approach clashed with Japanese norms. There’s no suggestion of intimidation, excessive humility, or reliance on reputation—only a misjudgment of cultural expectations.
---
Q3: How did Mark’s knowledge of his ancestors influence his second meeting?
Correct: It motivated him to research Japanese customs.
Distractors: It gave him a historical advantage / It made him more aggressive in negotiations / It distracted him from the main goal
Explanation: The text mentions that Mark wondered if his ancestral connection could help, but the key action was his decision to accommodate Japanese communication styles. There’s no evidence of historical leverage, aggression, or distraction—only a personal motivation to adapt.

Q4: Why was Mark’s second meeting more successful?
Correct: He adjusted his communication style to match their expectations.
Distractors: He used his company’s power to dominate the discussion / He focused only on affordable solutions / He ignored cultural differences entirely
Explanation: The success came from his broad compliments and indirect approach, which aligned with Japanese norms. The text explicitly states he avoided directness and prioritized cultural sensitivity, not corporate dominance, cost concerns, or ignoring differences.

Correct: Cultural sensitivity is more important than professional authority.
Distractors: Direct communication is always effective / Business success depends on historical ties / Silence in meetings is a sign of disrespect

Topic: a news article about the advantages and disadvantages of social media

Text:
Social media has become an integral part of modern life, offering countless benefits but also presenting significant challenges. One of its greatest advantages is the ability to connect people globally, breaking geographical barriers. For businesses, platforms like Instagram and Facebook serve as powerful tools for marketing, allowing brands to reach millions of potential customers with a single post. Many companies rely on social media sponsors to promote their products, often achieving impressive results. However, this convenience comes with drawbacks. The constant exposure to curated content can create unrealistic expectations, leading to feelings of inadequacy. Additionally, the curved path to success on social media—where algorithms favor certain posts—can make achievements seem predictable and even superficial.

Another issue is the spread of misinformation. False news can spread rapidly, often going viral before corrections are made. While some platforms impose penalties on accounts sharing harmful content, offensive remarks and hate speech remain an obstacle to creating a positive online environment. Despite these problems, social media also empowers individuals to raise awareness about important causes, making it a double-edged sword. The key lies in using these platforms responsibly, ensuring that the benefits outweigh the risks.
