Text:
Five years ago, I was a fool—working 12-hour days, skipping meals, and ignoring my family’s calls. My job in corporate expansion demanded everything, and I basically let it govern my life. My wife, a seamstress who worked with silk, often criticized my absence, but I dismissed her concerns, thinking success would come later.

Then, one evening, I saw a homeless man outside my office. He held a marker and scribbled on cardboard: "Time is the only thing you can’t buy back." That night, I realized I’d been chasing a prior version of myself—one who didn’t exist. The next morning, I quit my job.

At first, I struggled. My bank account shrank, and friends witnessed my panic. But I started small: I took weekends off, cooked dinner, and even joined a local theater group. Slowly, I learned to govern my time, not the other way around. Now, I work part-time, mentor young professionals, and my wife smiles when I come home early. Balance isn’t perfection—it’s choosing what basically matters.

Questions:
Q1: What does the narrator admit about his past self?
Correct: He admits he was reckless with his time.
Distractors: He admits he was always unhappy / He admits he ignored his wife’s career / He admits he never wanted success
Explanation: The narrator calls himself a "fool" and describes working excessively, skipping meals, and ignoring family—all signs of recklessness. He doesn’t say he was "always unhappy" (he only mentions stress later), nor does he ignore his wife’s career (she works as a seamstress). He clearly wanted success but misplaced priorities.
---
Q2: What role did the homeless man play in the narrator’s story?
Correct: He served as a catalyst for change.
Distractors: He offered the narrator a job / He was a former colleague / He represented the narrator’s future
Explanation: The man’s sign ("Time is the only thing you can’t buy back") directly triggered the narrator’s realization, leading to his resignation. There’s no mention of a job offer, a past connection, or the man symbolizing the narrator’s future—just a moment of reflection.
---
Q3: Why did the narrator’s wife criticize him?
Correct: Because he neglected his family for work.
Distractors: Because she wanted him to quit his job / Because he spent too much money / Because he refused to help with her silk work
Explanation: The text states she "often criticized" his absence and working long hours, implying she felt neglected. There’s no evidence she wanted him to quit immediately, that money was the issue, or that he refused to help with her work (she works independently).
---
Q4: What does the word "basically" emphasize in the phrase "I basically let it govern my life"?
Correct: That his work dominated his life in an extreme way.
Distractors: That he only let work control him sometimes / That he tried to balance work and life / That he followed rules strictly
Explanation: "Basically" here means "completely" or "to an extreme degree." The narrator admits work took over entirely, not partially or with balance. The other options contradict the text’s tone of overwork and neglect.
---
Q5: What lesson did the narrator learn from his experience?
Correct: True success requires prioritizing what truly matters.
Distractors: Money is the most important thing in life / Quitting your job is always the solution / Work-life balance is impossible to achieve
Explanation: The narrator’s conclusion is that balance comes from choosing "what basically matters" (family, time, mentoring), not material success. The text rejects the idea that money is supreme, that quitting is universal, or that balance is unattainable—he proves it’s possible through small changes.



Topic: a personal story about what I learned from mentoring someone

Text:
When I was asked to mentor a junior colleague, I expected a straightforward experience—teaching them the basics of project management. Instead, I found myself in a so-called "learning opportunity" that turned out to be far more controversial than I anticipated. My mentee, Jake, was brilliant but had a habit of making awkward decisions, like resigning from a key meeting without warning, which left our team scrambling.

One day, we were reviewing a proposal when Jake suggested cutting corners on ethics—something I found deeply unsettling. "But what if someone finds out?" I asked. He shrugged, saying, "It’s not weird if no one gets hurt." I realized then that his bunch of shortcuts weren’t just careless; they were part of a pattern. When I confronted him, he admitted he’d been burned before by a rat of a colleague who’d exposed his past mistakes.

Our mentorship became a lesson in boundaries. I learned that surrounding myself with people who shared my values was more important than just technical skills. Jake eventually left the company, but the experience taught me that mentoring isn’t just about teaching—it’s about recognizing when someone’s ethics don’t align with yours, even if it’s uncomfortable.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the author describe Jake’s suggestion to cut corners on ethics as "deeply unsettling"?
Correct: It challenged the author’s moral principles.
Distractors: It was a common practice in the workplace / It made the author question Jake’s intelligence / It was a legal violation.
Explanation: The author explicitly states the suggestion was "deeply unsettling," implying a conflict with their personal or professional ethics. The other options are unsupported: the text doesn’t call Jake’s intelligence into question, nor does it frame the suggestion as illegal (just unethical). The phrase "common practice" contradicts the author’s reaction.
---
Q2: What does the phrase "so-called 'learning opportunity'" suggest about the author’s initial expectations?
Correct: The author believed mentoring would be simpler than it turned out to be.
Distractors: The author thought Jake was already highly skilled / The author assumed the role would be temporary / The author expected Jake to resist guidance.
Explanation: The sarcastic tone of "so-called" implies the author underestimated the complexity of mentoring. The other options are incorrect: the text doesn’t mention Jake’s skill level, the role wasn’t described as temporary, and Jake didn’t resist guidance (he was the one making questionable decisions).
---
Q3: How does the author describe Jake’s behavior in the sentence "making awkward decisions"?
Correct: His choices were socially or professionally inappropriate.
Distractors: His decisions were illogical / His choices were risky but justified / His behavior was unpredictable.
Explanation: "Awkward" here refers to decisions that caused discomfort or problems (e.g., resigning without notice). The text doesn’t suggest illogic, justification, or unpredictability—just poor judgment. The rat analogy later reinforces that his actions were dishonest, not just erratic.
---
Q4: Why does the author mention Jake’s past experience with a "rat" of a colleague?
Correct: To explain why Jake was willing to compromise his ethics.
Distractors: To show Jake was vengeful / To prove Jake was a reliable source / To illustrate a common workplace issue.
Explanation: The "rat" reference reveals Jake’s past betrayal, which made him distrustful and more likely to take morally questionable shortcuts. The text doesn’t imply vengeance, reliability, or a broader workplace trend—just Jake’s personal motivation.
---
Q5: What lesson did the author learn from mentoring Jake about professional relationships?
Correct: Shared values are crucial for effective collaboration.
Distractors: Mentoring always leads to personal growth / Trust should be given without conditions / Junior colleagues should be managed strictly.
Explanation: The author concludes that surrounding themselves with people who share their ethics is vital. The other options are contradicted: the experience was frustrating, not universally growth-oriented; trust wasn’t blind; and the author didn’t advocate for strict control but for alignment on principles.



Topic: a personal account of a day I spent completely disconnected from technology

Text:
Last weekend, I decided to spend a full day without any technology—a rare experiment in modern life. I woke up early, walked to the outer edge of the city, and found myself standing in front of an old institution that had once been a library. The corridor leading inside was quiet, the air thick with the scent of aged books and wind drifting through the open windows.

I was curious about how people had functioned before smartphones. At one point, I even hesitated to ask a stranger for directions, fearing it might seem odd. But then I remembered my grandmother’s stories about relying on memory and observation. By midday, I had visited a local venue hosting a poetry reading, where the referee of the event—a retired teacher—spoke with such passion that I felt honoured to witness it.

The experience changed me significantly. Without constant notifications, I noticed details I’d usually miss: the way sunlight filtered through leaves, the rhythm of my own breathing. By evening, I realised how much technology had been distracting me from the present.

Distractors: To explain why the narrator chose this day for the experiment / To highlight the institution’s historical significance / To suggest the narrator was lost.
Explanation: The phrase "outer edge" implies a quiet, less populated area, contrasting with the usual busyness of city life. It does not explain the reason for the experiment (that comes later), nor does it focus on the institution’s history or the narrator’s disorientation.
---
Q2: What does the narrator’s hesitation to ask for directions reveal?
Correct: A self-consciousness about relying on others without technology.
Distractors: A fear of being judged by strangers / A lack of confidence in navigation skills / A desire to test their memory.
Explanation: The hesitation stems from the narrator’s awareness of how unusual it is to ask for help without digital aids. While "judged" or "lack of confidence" could be inferred, the text emphasizes the social awkwardness of the situation, not personal insecurity. The narrator doesn’t explicitly test their memory—it’s more about the cultural shift in communication.
---
Q3: How does the poetry reading contribute to the narrator’s experience?
Correct: It provided a meaningful, technology-free cultural experience.
Distractors: It proved that live events are more engaging than digital ones / It made the narrator feel nostalgic for the past / It distracted the narrator from their initial discomfort.
Explanation: The event is described as honouring the narrator’s presence, suggesting it was a fulfilling, immersive experience. Distractors: The building was poorly ventilated / The narrator was imagining the past / The wind was unusually strong that day.
Explanation: "Aged books" evoke history, while "wind" adds a fresh, natural contrast—both create atmosphere. There’s no indication of poor ventilation, and while nostalgia is implied, the narrator isn’t fantasizing. The wind isn’t described as strong; it’s part of the sensory details.
---
Q5: Why does the narrator feel "honoured" at the poetry reading?
Correct: Because the event felt authentic and deeply human.
Distractors: The referee was a famous figure / The venue was historically significant / The narrator had a personal connection to the poet.
Explanation: The text links the feeling of honour to the passion of the referee and the presence of the audience, suggesting a shared, meaningful experience. There’s no mention of the referee’s fame, the venue’s history, or a personal tie to the poet. The honour comes from the event itself, not external factors.



Topic: a sci-fi story about a society where human memory can be digitally stored and replayed

Text:
In the year 2147, the National Memory Archive had become as common as smartphones. Citizens could store their memories—joyful or painful—into digital files, replaying them later like holographic diaries. Lina, a former ballet dancer, had just launched her first memory session, reliving her master performance at the age of sixteen. The depth of emotion was overwhelming; she could still feel the wound in her ankle from a fall during rehearsal, though it had healed decades ago.

As she watched herself onstage, her younger self’s make-up flawless, Lina wondered if she should stand by her decision to erase the memory of her retirement. The Archive’s algorithms could reconstruct even the faintest details, but some experiences were too heavy to revisit. A technician emerged from the shadows, adjusting the neural interface. “You’re the first to request a national memory backup,” he said. “Most people just store personal moments.”

Lina hesitated. What if replaying her career too often blurred the line between past and present? The Archive promised clarity, but she feared losing the depth of her own emotions.

Correct: She fears losing the authenticity of her emotions.
Distractors: She regrets her decision to retire from ballet / She wants to erase the memory of her injury / She doubts the Archive’s technology works properly
Explanation: The text states Lina worries about “losing the depth of her own emotions,” implying she fears artificial reconstruction. She does not regret retirement (she relives her master performance with pride) or want to erase her injury (she acknowledges it as part of her past). The Archive’s functionality is not questioned—only the ethical depth of the experience.
---

Correct: Most people use it for personal, not historical, memories.
Distractors: The Archive is banned for national use / Citizens are required to store memories / Only dancers use the technology
Explanation: The technician notes Lina is the first to request a national memory backup, implying personal use is standard. There’s no mention of bans, requirements, or ballet exclusivity.

Correct: It includes physical sensations like pain.
Distractors: It lasts longer than usual / It requires special make-up / It’s stored in a different format
Explanation: The text specifies she “could still feel the wound in her ankle,” proving sensory depth. Duration, make-up, or storage formats are not discussed.

Distractors: It proves the Archive alters memories / It shows she was vain / It indicates the launch of a new product

Topic: an analysis of how food delivery apps have altered the social rituals of eating

Text:
The rise of food delivery apps has reshaped how we experience meals, turning a once classic social ritual into something faster but often lonelier. While these apps offer convenience—allowing us to order anything from sushi to acid-tested street food with a few taps—their function extends beyond mere efficiency. Studies suggest a growing tendency to eat alone, even when surrounded by others, as delivery culture prioritizes speed over shared meals. The exposure to diverse cuisines is undeniable, yet the goodness of a meal is no longer measured solely by taste but by how quickly it arrives. Critics argue that hidden fees and strict delivery windows add stress, making the experience feel transactional rather than nourishing. Some apps even offer "badges" for frequent users, turning consumption into a gamified habit. Is this progress, or a depressing erosion of the joy found in cooking and dining together?

Correct: It has become less communal due to food delivery apps.
Distractors: It is now more expensive than before / It relies heavily on traditional recipes / It has been replaced entirely by home cooking.
Explanation: The text states that delivery apps have made meals "faster but often lonelier," implying a decline in shared dining. The other options are unsupported: cost (fees are mentioned but not as the main issue), traditional recipes (not discussed), or replacement by home cooking (delivery apps encourage eating out).
---

Correct: They prioritize speed over the social experience of eating.
Distractors: They only deliver unhealthy food / They require users to cook at home / They eliminate the need for restaurants entirely.
Explanation: The text explains that delivery apps focus on "speed over shared meals," making the experience less social. The other options are incorrect: the text doesn’t claim all delivered food is unhealthy, nor does it suggest users must cook; restaurants still exist alongside delivery.

Q3: How does the text describe the tendency associated with food delivery apps?
Correct: People are more likely to eat alone despite being with others.
Distractors: Users prefer cooking at home instead of ordering / Delivery apps encourage group dining / Most customers avoid ordering spicy or acid foods.
Explanation: The text notes a "growing tendency to eat alone," even in social settings. The other options contradict the text: delivery apps reduce home cooking, not encourage it; they don’t promote group dining; and acid foods are mentioned as an example of variety, not avoidance.
---
Q4: What does the text imply about the goodness of a meal in the delivery era?
Correct: It is now evaluated based on delivery speed as well as taste.

Explanation: The text states that meal quality is judged by "how quickly it arrives," not just taste. The other options are incorrect: reputation isn’t mentioned, quality controls aren’t discussed, and customers still value flavor (the text critiques the addition of speed as a factor, not its replacement).

Distractors: It guarantees free delivery for loyal customers / It discourages users from ordering frequently / It replaces traditional loyalty programs in restaurants.
Explanation: The text describes badges as part of a "gamified habit," implying they encourage repetitive ordering. The other options are incorrect: badges don’t guarantee free delivery (hidden fees are the focus), they don’t discourage ordering, and the text doesn’t compare them to restaurant loyalty programs.



Topic: a story about a long-standing rivalry that transforms into mutual respect

Text:
For decades, Captain Elias Voss and Commander Lina Kova had been rivals in the space exploration program. Their rivalry began during a high-stakes mission when a critical malfunction threatened the entire Aurora crew. Voss, as mission lead, made a decision that saved the ship but left Kova’s research module exposed to dangerous radiation. Though the crew was saved, the indication of negligence haunted Kova, who publicly criticized Voss’s conduct in post-mission briefings. The affair became legendary—two brilliant minds locked in a battle of pride and protocol.

Years later, both were assigned to lead the Eclipse expedition, a daring voyage into the uncharted darkness of the Oort Cloud. As the ship neared a massive ice comet, sensors detected a probable collision course. Voss, now more cautious, proposed an evasive maneuver, but Kova argued it would waste fuel. Tensions flared until the crew pointed out a flaw in both plans: the comet’s trajectory was unstable, and their obstacle was time itself. In a rare moment of unity, they combined their strategies—Voss’s precision with Kova’s adaptability—to relieve the ship of its deadly path.

By the mission’s end, neither sought credit. Instead, they stood on the observation deck, regard for each other evident in their silence. The rivalry had not vanished, but it had transformed. "You were right about the radiation shields," Kova admitted. Voss smirked. "And you were right about fuel efficiency." The crew watched, amused, as the two legends of space exploration finally acknowledged what they’d both known for years: alone, they were brilliant; together, they were unstoppable.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text suggest about the initial conflict between Voss and Kova?
Correct: It was rooted in a mission-related mistake that damaged Kova’s work.
Distractors: It stemmed from a personal dislike between them / It was caused by a crew member’s betrayal / It began when they competed for the same promotion.
Explanation: The conflict arose when Voss’s decision exposed Kova’s research to radiation, which she saw as negligence. There’s no mention of personal dislike, betrayal, or a promotion competition—only a professional disagreement tied to a specific incident.
---
Q2: Why does the text describe the Aurora mission as "high-stakes"?
Correct: The malfunction risked the entire crew’s safety.
Distractors: It required an unprecedented level of teamwork / The mission was the first to explore the Oort Cloud / The crew had to choose between two equally dangerous options.
Explanation: The term "high-stakes" is used because the malfunction threatened the crew’s lives. While teamwork was involved, the focus is on the life-or-death urgency. The Oort Cloud is mentioned later, and no two equally dangerous options are presented—only Voss’s decision and its consequences.
---
Q3: How does the text portray the Eclipse expedition as different from the Aurora mission?
Correct: It forced Voss and Kova to collaborate despite their rivalry.
Distractors: It was less dangerous than the previous mission / It allowed them to prove their individual skills / The crew had more time to prepare for challenges.
Explanation: The Eclipse mission highlights their forced cooperation to solve a shared problem. It’s not less dangerous (the comet was a threat), nor does it emphasize individual skills—rather, their combined approach is key. The text doesn’t mention preparation time as a factor.
---
Q4: What role does the comet’s "unstable trajectory" play in the resolution?
Correct: It made both Voss’s and Kova’s initial plans insufficient.
Distractors: It forced the crew to abandon the mission entirely / It revealed that only Voss’s strategy could work / It proved Kova’s criticism of Voss was justified.
Explanation: The instability of the comet’s path invalidated both leaders’ original solutions, compelling them to merge their ideas. The crew didn’t abandon the mission, nor does the text suggest one plan was superior—only that neither worked alone.
---
Q5: What does the final scene on the observation deck symbolize?
Correct: The end of their rivalry and the beginning of mutual respect.
Distractors: Their quiet acceptance of their shared legacy / A temporary truce before renewed conflict / The crew’s relief that the mission was over.
Explanation: The silence and acknowledgment ("You were right...") mark a shift from rivalry to respect. While they do reflect on their legacy, the focus is on their changed dynamic. There’s no indication of a temporary truce or crew relief—only the two leaders’ personal realization.



Topic: a story about a person who moves to a new country every few years and loves it

Text:
Lena had always dreamed of living abroad, and at 32, she had finally embraced the life of a global nomad. Every few years, she packed her bags and moved to a new country—first to Japan for a revision session at a language academy, then to Brazil for a session with a cultural analyst studying urban migration. Her latest move had been to Portugal, where she rented a small apartment in Lisbon’s historic center. The hidden charm of the city’s narrow streets and the warmth of its people made her fall in love all over again.

At first, adjusting to a new routine was always the hardest phase. She missed her old friends and struggled with the language barrier, but she never blamed herself for long. Instead, she threw herself into learning Portuguese, attending evening classes, and exploring wherever her curiosity led her. The initial culture shock faded quickly, replaced by excitement. She loved how each country reshaped her perspective, teaching her to see the world differently.

One evening, after a long day of job hunting, Lena sat on a bench near the river, watching the sunset. She realized that no matter how many times she moved, she always found something to love. The origin of her restlessness was simple: she thrived on change. As she smiled to herself, she knew this strike of wanderlust would never fade.

Distractors: She is forced to relocate due to her job / She dislikes her current country and wants to escape / She follows a strict personal challenge to visit every continent
Explanation: The text states she "embraced the life of a global nomad" and "loved how each country reshaped her perspective," showing she seeks cultural enrichment. There is no mention of job-related relocation, dislike for her current country, or a continent-visiting challenge.

Distractors: She avoids social interactions to prevent culture shock / She relies on her previous experiences to cope quickly / She complains about the lack of familiar routines
Explanation: The text describes her attending evening classes and exploring wherever curiosity leads, indicating active engagement. She does not avoid social interactions, nor does she complain—she embraces change.

Q3: What does the word "hidden" in "the hidden charm of the city’s narrow streets" suggest about Lena’s perspective?
Correct: She appreciates the subtle, less obvious qualities of the city.
Distractors: She finds the city’s streets dangerous or difficult to navigate / She believes the city’s beauty is only visible to locals / She dislikes the traditional architecture of the area
Explanation: "Hidden charm" implies she values the understated, unique aspects of Lisbon, not its dangers or exclusivity. The text does not suggest she dislikes the architecture or that beauty is local-only.

Q4: How does Lena feel about her frequent moves after some time?

Distractors: She regrets her decision and wants to settle down / She feels lonely despite making new friends / She believes the moves have negatively affected her career

Topic: a personal account of a time I had to make a split-second decision

Text:
Last summer, I made a split-second decision that still haunts me. I had just bought a second-hand car—a licence to drive it was the only thing missing. The seller, a charming man named Leo, insisted it was a great investment, but something felt off. His hands trembled slightly when he handed over the keys, and his story about the car’s history kept changing. Still, I was desperate for transport, so I ignored my instincts.

As I drove home, the car’s engine sputtered. I pulled over, but before I could check, a police officer approached. Through my rear-view lens, I saw his face harden. My heart pounded—had Leo been running a fraud? The officer asked for my papers. My hands shook as I realised I’d left my temporary driving licence at home. Panic set in.

Then, I noticed the officer’s radio crackling. A call came in about a stolen car matching my description. My mind raced. Should I confess? Or lie and risk arrest? In that zone of shocking realisation, I made my choice. I showed him my registration documents—real ones I’d kept in the glove compartment—and explained the engine trouble. He hesitated, then let me go with a warning.

Later, I learned Leo had been involved in a fraud ring. My quick thinking saved me from a nightmare. The car was impounded, but I kept the lesson: never rush an investment, no matter the pace of life. Even with medication for my anxiety, that day taught me to trust my gut over convenience.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator initially ignore their instincts about the car?
Correct: They were desperate for transportation.
Distractors: They trusted Leo completely / They believed the car was genuinely cheap / They had no other options at the time.
Explanation: The text states, “I was desperate for transport,” which explains why the narrator ignored red flags. “Trusted Leo completely” is false—they mention “something felt off.” “Believed the car was genuinely cheap” is unsupported; the focus is on urgency, not price. “Had no other options” is partially true but less precise than “desperate,” which directly answers why they ignored instincts.
---
Q2: What detail about the officer’s behaviour made the narrator most nervous?
Correct: His radio call about a stolen car matching their description.
Distractors: His face hardening when he approached / His request for the narrator’s papers / His decision to give a warning instead of arresting them.
Explanation: The shocking realisation came when the officer’s radio mentioned a stolen car (“A call came in about a stolen car matching my description”). His facial expression (“his face harden”) was alarming but not the decisive moment. Requesting papers was routine; the warning came after the narrator’s quick thinking, not before.
---
Q3: How did the narrator’s medication relate to the story?
Correct: It highlighted their tendency to rush decisions despite health concerns.
Distractors: It prevented them from panicking during the encounter / It was the reason they trusted Leo / It caused them to make a reckless choice.
Explanation: The narrator mentions “Even with medication for my anxiety,” implying they usually manage stress but still rushed. It doesn’t prevent panic (“my hands shook”), justify trusting Leo, or cause recklessness—rather, it contrasts their usual caution with this impulsive act.
---
Q4: What does the word “zone” (paragraph 3) most likely refer to?
Correct: A mental state of panic and indecision.
Distractors: A physical area near the police officer / A specific location where the car broke down / A legal grey area regarding the car’s ownership.
Explanation: The “zone” follows “that day taught me to trust my gut,” describing the narrator’s “shocking realisation” and “panic” (“In that zone of shocking realisation”). It’s not a physical place (no location is described) or a legal concept (the focus is on the narrator’s psychology).
---
Q5: Which of the following best describes the narrator’s final lesson?
Correct: Rushing decisions can lead to irreversible consequences.
Distractors: Always verify second-hand purchases with a lawyer / Quick thinking can override bad instincts / Trusting your gut is more important than convenience.
Explanation: The narrator concludes “never rush an investment, no matter the pace of life,” linking haste to regret (“that day taught me”). “Verify with a lawyer” is unsupported; “quick thinking can override bad instincts” contradicts the text (they didn’t override instincts—they ignored them initially). “Trust your gut” is true but incomplete; the lesson emphasises avoiding rashness, not just trusting instincts.



Topic: a story about an experience that completely changed my perspective on life

Text:
The turning point in my life came when I was bound for a routine job interview at a local museum. I had spent years studying history, but I was partly skeptical about whether my degree would lead to anything meaningful. The interview was just another step in what felt like an extension of my predictable routine—until I saw the job description.

The position required not just knowledge but creativity in interpreting historical documents. The interviewer, a passionate historian, asked me to analyze a poorly preserved letter from the 19th century. Instead of focusing on facts, he encouraged me to imagine the emotions behind the words. My initial speculation was that the task was impossible, but his enthusiasm gave me an unexpected incentive to think differently.

As I spoke, I realized how much I had been limiting myself. My rigid focus on memorization had never given me a chance to explore history’s human side. The interview wasn’t just about getting the job—it was a boost to my confidence. I left feeling that my perspective on learning had been too narrow. A few weeks later, I was elected for the role, but the real change was in how I saw my future. No longer was I a passive spectator of history; I wanted to be part of its storytelling.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the narrator mention that the job interview was "just another step in what felt like an extension of my predictable routine"?
Correct: To emphasize how unexpected the interview’s impact on his life would be.
Distractors: To explain why he was nervous about the interview / To suggest he had no interest in the job / To describe his lack of experience in public speaking
Explanation: The phrase highlights the contrast between the narrator’s initial expectations (a routine event) and the actual outcome (a life-changing experience). It doesn’t address nerves, disinterest, or public speaking skills, which aren’t mentioned.
---
Q2: What does the interviewer’s focus on "emotions behind the words" suggest about his teaching method?

Topic: a story about reconnecting with someone after many years of silence

Text:
The old publication warehouse in the regional capital had once been a bustling hub, but now it stood half-empty, its shelves lined with forgotten books. Among them was a rare edition of Lord of the Rings—a bargain at just five euros, though its pages were yellowed with age. Daniel had spent years avoiding this place, but fate, or perhaps nostalgia, led him back. He had come to incorporate a few forgotten treasures into his new bookstore, though he never expected to find her there.

The woman behind the counter, now silver-haired, looked up as he approached. "You’ve grown," she said, her voice steady but warm. Daniel froze. It was Clara, his childhood friend, the one he had abandoned without a word after that risky summer trip. The distribution of blame had always been grade-A complicated between them, but now, years later, he needed to satisfy his curiosity—or maybe his guilt. "I didn’t think you’d still be here," he admitted, his hands trembling slightly as he held out the book. "This was yours, wasn’t it?"

Clara smiled faintly. "Some things are too precious to let go." She studied him for a long moment before speaking again. "You left like it was an invasion of your privacy. No letter, no call." Daniel swallowed hard. The silence between them had been a wall, but now, with the weight of time pressing down, he wondered if they could rebuild what was broken—or if some bridges were meant to stay burned.

Topic: a story about a time I stood up for something I believed in despite opposition

Text:

The principal argued it was "too controversial," but I knew the band’s music was about potential—breaking norms and giving a voice to students who felt ignored. I had spent months organizing the event, and this seemed like a betrayal of the investment we’d all made.

I gathered my friends, and together we drafted a petition. We argued that the band’s expertise in activism made them the perfect choice to address additional concerns about student freedom. The principal dismissed us, saying, "You’re just kids—you don’t understand the fault in your logic." But I refused to back down. I took the step of publicly defending the band in the school newspaper, listing every reason why their music mattered.

At first, the opposition was fierce. Teachers warned me it would hurt my grades; parents called me "reckless." But I stood my ground, using facts—surveys showing students wanted diverse music, letters from local bands supporting us. In the end, the school relented. The band played, and the festival became the most talked-about event in years. I learned that even when you’re outnumbered, stuff like passion and persistence can change minds.

A group of elderly residents had been organizing a fundraiser to pay off a debt from renovating the building’s deck, which had become unsafe. The residents, though divided at first, quickly found unity in their outrage. Some even suggested boycotting future council meetings, but others argued for a more peaceful approach, like petitioning for transparency.

I decided to stay and help, even though I had planned to leave early. The situation reminded me of how fragile sustainable community efforts can be when faced with bureaucratic indifference. By the end of the day, we had drafted a letter to the council, and I left feeling both angry and determined to keep fighting for what was right.

That night, I sat on my balcony, watching the jets take off into the sky, and knew I couldn’t continue. The job had once been my passion, but now it felt like a prison. I quit the next morning, and though I feared judgment, I realized something moreover important: staying would have hurt me more in the long run.

At first, I was skeptical. The space was dimly lit, and the pieces looked chaotic—until I noticed how the light played on a sculpture made of crumpled metal sheets. A protester’s sign nearby read, "Art should challenge, not just decorate." I realized the artist had spent months crafting this, turning waste into something meaningful. The delivery of emotion was raw, unlike the polished canvases I usually admired.

Nowadays, I see street art differently. I even bought a print of a mural I once thought was ridiculous. The experience made me conscious of how quickly judgments can blind us to beauty.

Topic: a personal story about the moment I understood a piece of advice I had ignored for years

I took a neutral stance, focused on efficiency, and trusted my team’s creativity. The project succeeded, and I understood: overthinking was the real risk.

Topic: a story about a time I broke a family tradition and the consequences

My mother, though hurt, eventually forgave me—but only after I apologized and promised to attend the next debate. The confusion in my mind about priorities had led to a rift I hadn’t anticipated. Even my sibling, usually supportive, struggled to perceive my actions as anything but disrespectful. The experience taught me that traditions aren’t just habits; they’re bridges between generations, and breaking them without thought can leave gaps no apology can instantly fill.

Topic: a speculative story set in a world where lying has become physically impossible

Text:
In the year 2147, the world had changed. After the Great Truth Serum was released—a drug that made lying physically impossible—society transformed overnight. Governments collapsed, corporations specialized in truth verification, and even casual conversations became painfully honest. The border between nations blurred as people crossed freely, no longer bound by political lies.

At the heart of this new era stood the Truth Enforcement Bar, a casual yet intimidating establishment where dissenters were brought to infer the consequences of deception. Inside, a troop of former lawyers, now trained in psychological profiling, would question suspects until they confessed—or their bodies instantly rejected the serum’s side effects. "You can’t hide behind words anymore," said Dr. Elara Voss, the bar’s lead analyst, as she adjusted her glasses. "Every lie is a litre of poison in your veins."

Opposition groups, like the Silent Majority, claimed the serum was a tool of control. But most citizens welcomed the change. No more broken promises, no more hidden agendas—just raw, unfiltered truth. Even criminals instantly admitted their crimes, their faces twisting in shame as the serum forced honesty from them. The world was cleaner, but the cost was a society where deception was as dangerous as fire.

Topic: a personal reflection on what it means to live a good life outside the metrics of productivity

Text:
What does it mean to live a good life? For centuries, philosophers and thinkers have tried to define it, often citing productivity, wealth, or success as key measures. But in today’s world, where technology is moving at a rapid pace and society values efficiency above all else, we risk forgetting something crucial: happiness isn’t always tied to achievement.

My origin story of rethinking this began in my early twenties. Fresh out of university, I was convincing myself that climbing the corporate ladder was the only path to fulfillment. I spent years chasing promotions, grade-based rewards, and the approval of others. But one day, after a mass layoff left me jobless and exhausted, I realized something: I had defeated myself. Not by failing, but by losing sight of what truly mattered. The value of my life wasn’t in the hours I worked or the titles I earned—it was in the quiet moments I had ignored: a sunset watched from a park bench, a deep conversation with a friend, or simply breathing without guilt.

Society often measures worth by output, but real richness lies in presence. It’s okay to slow down. To value rest, curiosity, and connection over constant motion. The good life isn’t about winning the race—it’s about choosing how you run it.

Topic: an essay on the psychological impact of doomscrolling and the need for a new digital literacy

Text:
In today’s digital age, the habit of doomscrolling—endlessly consuming negative news—has become a pure epidemic. While it may seem harmless, research shows it has severe psychological consequences, from increased anxiety to chronic stress. The problem is evident: our brains are wired to focus on threats, and constant exposure to bad news exploits this instinct. Yet, many users remain unaware of the harm, treating doomscrolling as a passive, even sustainable pastime.

Psychologists and digital therapists warn that this behavior spoils mental well-being, much like a feather in the wind—seemingly light, yet capable of shifting one’s mood entirely. The irony? Most people do not see themselves as opponents of their own habits. Instead, they justify scrolling as a way to "stay informed," ignoring the lack of adequate boundaries. Social media algorithms, designed to maximize engagement, feed users a diet of outrage, further deepening the cycle.

The solution lies in developing a new form of digital literacy—one that teaches users to recognize harmful patterns and seek feedback on their consumption habits. Just as pilots train to handle emergencies, we must learn to "land" our devices when needed. Without intervention, doomscrolling will continue to hijack our attention, leaving us in a state of perpetual unease—like an aircraft stuck in turbulence with no clear path to smooth skies.

Questions:
Q1: What does the author imply about the psychological effects of doomscrolling?
Correct: They are often underestimated by users.
Distractors: They are easily reversible with willpower / They primarily affect older generations / They are a deliberate strategy by social media companies
Explanation: The text states that users treat doomscrolling as "harmless" and "sustainable," implying they underestimate its impact ("many users remain unaware of the harm"). The other options are incorrect: the text does not claim willpower is sufficient ("psychologists warn"), it does not target older generations, and while algorithms exploit the habit, the focus is on user behavior, not corporate intent.

Distractors: It is as light as a feather, making it easy to ignore / It physically weighs down the mind / It symbolizes fragility in digital habits
Explanation: The text describes the feather as "seemingly light, yet capable of shifting one’s mood entirely," emphasizing the hidden severity of doomscrolling. The other options misrepresent the metaphor: it’s not about physical weight or fragility but the contrast between appearance and effect.
---
Q3: Why does the author mention aircraft in turbulence?
Correct: To illustrate a state of uncontrolled distress.
Distractors: To compare doomscrolling to flying / To suggest users need a pilot’s training / To argue that digital habits are unpredictable
Explanation: The turbulence metaphor highlights "perpetual unease" and lack of control, aligning with the psychological chaos of doomscrolling. The other options are incorrect: the focus isn’t on flying itself, nor is it about needing training (though literacy is suggested), but about the feeling of instability.
---
Q4: What role do social media algorithms play in doomscrolling?
Correct: They exacerbate the problem by prioritizing engagement.
Distractors: They are the primary cause of anxiety / They are designed to educate users / They provide a neutral platform for news

Correct: Developing digital literacy to set boundaries.

Explanation: The text emphasizes "a new form of digital literacy" and "seeking feedback" to manage habits, not therapy (though therapists are mentioned as observers), censorship, or blanket avoidance of digital tools. The solution is proactive and skill-based, not restrictive.



Topic: an analysis of the psychological principles behind effective user interface design

Text:
Designing an effective user interface (UI) relies heavily on psychological principles that shape how users interact with digital products. A senior researcher at a tech company once conducted a thesis-based survey to explore these principles, revealing fascinating findings. One key insight is the role of short-term memory in UI design. Users process information quickly, so designers must prioritize clarity and simplicity. For example, placing critical material (like buttons or navigation menus) in proportion to the screen’s layout reduces cognitive shock and improves usability.

Another principle involves the brain’s organization of visual information. Studies show that users scan interfaces in predictable patterns, often focusing on the top-left corner first. Ignoring this can lead to frustration. Additionally, gamification techniques—such as progress bars or achievement badges—leverage the brain’s reward system, making tasks feel less tedious. Even small details, like color contrast or typography, influence user perception, proving that UI design is as much about psychology as it is about aesthetics.

Questions:
Q1: What was the primary focus of the senior researcher’s thesis?
Correct: The psychological principles behind UI design
Distractors: The impact of gamification on user engagement / The technical challenges of coding interfaces / The historical evolution of digital interfaces
Explanation: The text explicitly states the researcher’s work explored "psychological principles that shape how users interact with digital products." While gamification is mentioned, it is a subset of the broader focus, not the primary subject. The thesis did not address technical coding challenges or historical evolution.
---
Q2: Why does the text mention the proportion of UI elements?
Correct: To explain how layout affects cognitive processing
Distractors: To argue that larger screens are always better / To suggest that users prefer symmetrical designs / To criticize modern UI trends
Explanation: The text links proportion to reducing cognitive shock and improving usability by aligning with how users process information. The other options are unsupported—screen size, symmetry, or criticism of trends are not discussed.
---
Q3: What does the text imply about the brain’s role in UI design?
Correct: Users rely on predictable visual patterns for efficiency
Distractors: The brain ignores irrelevant UI elements automatically / Users prefer complex interfaces for stimulation / The brain’s reward system is irrelevant to task completion
Explanation: The text notes that users scan interfaces predictably (e.g., top-left focus), which designers must account for. The brain does not ignore irrelevant elements (contradicting the first distractor), nor does complexity improve engagement (second distractor). The reward system is relevant (third distractor is incorrect).
---
Q4: How does the survey’s finding about short-term memory influence design?
Correct: Designers should minimize cognitive load by simplifying interfaces
Distractors: Users remember all details if presented clearly / Complex interfaces enhance memory retention / Short-term memory has no impact on UI preferences
Explanation: The text states users process information quickly, so designers must prioritize clarity. The other options contradict this: users don’t retain all details, complexity harms usability, and short-term memory does influence design.
---
Q5: What is the relationship between material and UI effectiveness?
Correct: Critical elements must be visually accessible to reduce user frustration
Distractors: The material used (e.g., plastic vs. glass) affects usability / Users prefer interfaces with minimal material elements / The material’s color is the most important factor
Explanation: The text defines material as functional components (e.g., buttons) that must be prioritized for usability. The other options misinterpret material as physical properties, minimalism, or color dominance, none of which are supported.

Atmosphere is just as critical as the food. The dim lighting, mismatched vintage chairs, and walls lined with old recipe books create a space that feels both intimate and intellectual. Whether you’re here for the food or the philosophy, The Hollow Spoon leaves you questioning what dining should be.

Topic: a reflective essay on the experience of impostor syndrome in a new professional or social role

Text:
When I accepted the role of editor for the Grand Review magazine’s annual edition, I was delighted—and terrified. The position was a dream, but the weight of expectation shaped my first days like a tragedy in slow motion. Every decision felt like a gamble, and I questioned whether I was truly qualified. This was impostor syndrome, a component of self-doubt that had haunted me before but now loomed larger than ever.

The foundation of my fear was simple: I didn’t feel like the chair of a prestigious publication. In meetings, I second-guessed my ideas, even when colleagues praised them. Why did they say that? I wondered. Do they know I’m faking it? Furthermore, small mistakes—like misplacing a comma in an author’s submission—felt like personal failures, magnified by the grand scale of the project.

One evening, after a particularly grueling day, I sat at my desk, staring at a bat of proofs. The irony wasn’t lost on me: I’d spent years admiring editors from afar, and now I was one. But the doubt persisted. Was I really cut out for this? The answer, I realized, wasn’t in perfection but in progress. Over time, the syndrome softened, though it never vanished entirely. Now, I see it as a reminder that even the most accomplished people feel the same way.

Questions:
Q1: What does the author initially feel about accepting the editor role?
Correct: Delighted—and terrified.
Distractors: Overwhelmed by excitement / Relieved to finally prove herself / Confident in her abilities
Explanation: The text states, “I was delighted—and terrified,” directly describing the author’s mixed emotions. “Overwhelmed by excitement” is incorrect because the author also felt fear. “Relieved to finally prove herself” is unsupported—the text emphasizes doubt, not relief. “Confident in her abilities” contradicts the self-doubt described.
---
Q2: How does the author describe the impact of impostor syndrome on her early days in the role?
Correct: It made her question her qualifications constantly.
Distractors: It made her avoid meetings entirely / It made her seek constant reassurance / It made her ignore feedback from colleagues
Explanation: The author writes, “Every decision felt like a gamble, and I questioned whether I was truly qualified,” showing persistent self-doubt. “Avoid meetings entirely” is false—she attended them but second-guessed herself. “Seek constant reassurance” is not mentioned. “Ignore feedback” contradicts the text, which implies she was overly sensitive to criticism.
---
Q3: What does the author compare the weight of her expectations to?
Correct: A tragedy in slow motion.
Distractors: A storm approaching / A burden she couldn’t lift / A race against time
Explanation: The text explicitly states, “the weight of expectation shaped my first days like a tragedy in slow motion.” The other options are not referenced: no storm, no physical burden, and no time pressure is described.
---
Q4: What does the author realize about impostor syndrome over time?
Correct: It’s a reminder that even accomplished people feel the same way.
Distractors: It disappears completely with experience / It’s a sign of weakness / It only affects beginners
Explanation: The author concludes, “Now, I see it as a reminder that even the most accomplished people feel the same way,” rejecting the idea that it’s unique to her. “Disappears completely” is false—she admits it persists. “Sign of weakness” is contradicted by her reflection on others experiencing it too. “Only affects beginners” is unsupported.

The other options are incorrect: the text doesn’t suggest she expected it to be easier, nor does it imply past experience would help. “Assumed impostor syndrome wouldn’t affect her” is false—she describes it as a persistent struggle.

As the town’s mayor stood on the cliffs overlooking the ocean, she wondered if humanity would ever achieve true energy independence—or if they were doomed to forever chase a breakthrough just out of reach.

Topic: a story about a portrait painter who realizes they are capturing the subject's soul

Text:
Eleanor Hart had spent twenty years as a portrait painter, but her work had never truly satisfied her—until the day she met Daniel Mercer. A newly promoted professor of philosophy, Daniel was known for his sharp mind and even sharper wit. When Eleanor was commissioned to paint his portrait, she hesitated. His reputation was high, and she feared she could not capture his essence. But the client insisted, and Eleanor dared to accept.

The first session was disastrous. The lighting was incorrect, casting harsh shadows across Daniel’s face, and his expressions seemed forced. Eleanor nearly resigned from the project, convinced she lacked the skill. Yet, something unique happened when she returned the next week. As she studied his eyes, she noticed a flicker of emotion—something deeper than thought. His left hand, resting on the armrest, trembled slightly, as if an organ within him was responding to unseen forces.

Eleanor adjusted her brushstrokes, focusing not on his features but on the energy radiating from him. The portrait took shape, but it was unlike anything she had ever created. Daniel’s soul seemed to glow through the paint, his intelligence and vulnerability intertwined. When she unveiled the finished piece, the gallery fell silent. Even Daniel, usually unshaken, stared in awe. "It’s stunning," he whispered. "You’ve captured more than my face—you’ve captured me."

Topic: a psychological mystery about a person who finds a photograph of themselves from a school they never attended

Text:
Liam had always prided himself on his independence. At 32, he had never relied on anyone for emotional support, preferring solitude to exposure to others’ opinions. So when he stumbled upon a fabulous old photograph in a secondhand bookshop, he was stunned. The image showed him—wearing a school uniform he didn’t recognize—standing beside a former classmate he’d never met. The caption read: "Class of 2003, St. Aldwin’s Academy."

Liam’s hands trembled as he examined the distribution of faces in the group photo. His own was unmistakable, yet the school’s name meant nothing to him. He had no childhood memories of the place, no booking in his parents’ diaries, not even a tragic story about a lost sibling who might have attended. His wage as a freelance graphic designer barely covered rent, let alone therapy, but the photo haunted him. Was it a cruel joke? A healthcare scam? Or proof of a life he’d repressed?

The more Liam researched, the stranger it became. St. Aldwin’s had burned down years ago, its records destroyed. His target—the truth—seemed impossible to hit. Yet the photo’s existence nagged at him. Had he been adopted? Swapped at birth? Or was his mind playing tricks, a side effect of the exposure to stress he’d ignored for years?

Explanation: The phrase suggests Liam was methodically examining the photo, likely to verify whether the face in the uniform was truly his. Explanation: The fire’s destruction of records makes it nearly impossible for Liam to confirm whether he attended, reinforcing the psychological mystery. Explanation: The text states his "wage barely covered rent," implying he lacks funds for expensive solutions like therapy or private research. Topic: an adventure story about teenagers who find themselves inside a computer game

Text:
Liam and Mia were conservative gamers—preferring realistic simulations over fantasy worlds. So when they stumbled upon GlobeQuest, a new open-world game, they hesitated. "It looks genuine," Mia whispered, adjusting her wrist band to log in. The game promised an immersive experience, but neither expected to get isolated inside it.

Minutes later, their avatars stood in a digital forest, the air humming with unfamiliar sounds. "This isn’t just a game," Liam muttered as trees surrounded them, their leaves shimmering with an eerie glow. A notification flashed: "Warning: System breach detected. Override protocol activated." Before they could react, their status bars flickered—red, then gray. The game had trapped them.

Panic set in. Mia’s parents had taken an overseas trip, leaving her mortgage payments piling up. "We need to find a way out," she said, gripping Liam’s arm. A nearby NPC chopped wood with a rusted axe. "Help?" Mia called. The figure turned, its eyes blank. "You’re not real," Liam realized. The game had no exit—only endless levels.

Topic: a story about a difficult ethical dilemma I faced

Text:
Last winter, I faced one of the most difficult ethical dilemmas of my life. It happened during a charity event where I was volunteering alongside a team of doctors. A young man, barely conscious, was brought in after a severe accident. His clothes were torn, and his arm was bent at an unnatural angle—clearly broken. The doctors urged us to act fast, but the situation was far from predictable.

The man was naked from the waist up, and his condition was critical. The hospital was crowded, and the only available stretcher was narrow, barely fitting one person. Another patient, an elderly woman, was waiting nearby, also in urgent need of care. The doctors hesitated, torn between treating the young man first or risking his life by waiting for a wider stretcher.

I felt an overwhelming urge to do something, but the dilemma was dynamic—every second counted. Should we prioritize the young man, knowing the stretcher might not be enough, or delay his treatment to ensure he got proper care? In the end, we chose to stabilize him immediately, even if it meant improvising with the narrow stretcher. It was a decision I still struggle with today.

Questions:
Q1: What does the narrator describe as the most challenging aspect of the situation?
Correct: The urgency of the decision
Distractors: The physical condition of the patients / The lack of medical supplies / The emotional stress of the volunteers
Explanation: The narrator emphasizes the "urgent" nature of the situation ("act fast," "every second counted") and the difficulty of choosing between two critical patients. The physical condition is described but not the most challenging aspect—the focus is on the ethical dilemma. The text does not mention a lack of supplies, and while emotional stress may have been present, it is not the central conflict.
---
Q2: Why does the narrator mention that the stretcher was "narrow"?
Correct: To highlight the difficulty of the decision
Distractors: To explain why the young man was in pain / To show that the hospital was poorly equipped / To suggest that the stretcher was unsafe
Explanation: The stretcher’s narrowness symbolizes the limited options available, making the choice harder. It does not directly cause the young man’s pain (his injury is already severe) or prove the hospital was poorly equipped (the issue is about prioritization, not resources). The stretcher’s safety is not questioned—it was the only option, not an unsafe one.
---
Q3: What does the word "hesitate" suggest about the doctors' reaction?
Correct: They were unsure how to proceed
Distractors: They were afraid to make a mistake / They lacked confidence in their skills / They were waiting for someone else to decide
Explanation: "Hesitate" implies doubt or indecision, not fear or incompetence. The doctors were torn between two valid options, not paralyzed by fear or delegation. The text does not state they were waiting for others—it describes their internal conflict.
---
Q4: How does the narrator describe the ethical dilemma as "dynamic"?
Correct: It required immediate, evolving decisions
Distractors: It involved multiple changing factors / It was influenced by external pressures / It had unpredictable consequences
Explanation: "Dynamic" here means the situation was fast-moving and required quick, adaptive choices ("every second counted"). While external pressures (e.g., the crowd) and unpredictable consequences (e.g., outcomes of the choice) may have been present, the primary meaning is the urgency and fluidity of the decision-making process.
---
Q5: What does the narrator imply about their own role in the decision?
Correct: They felt personally responsible for the outcome
Distractors: They acted without consulting others / They regretted not choosing differently / They believed the doctors made the wrong choice
Explanation: The phrase "a decision I still struggle with today" suggests personal guilt or reflection, not regret over the specific choice made. The narrator does not claim they acted alone (they were "alongside" the team) or criticize the doctors’ judgment. The focus is on their own moral burden, not the doctors’ errors.



Topic: a personal story about the moment I realized I was no longer a child

Text:
The summer I turned twelve, my family moved from the city to a small farm on the outskirts of town, where my grandfather had been a settler decades earlier. At first, I was thrilled—imagining myself as a pioneer, helping with the dairy cows and planting vegetables. But reality hit hard when I realized I couldn’t even fix a broken fence post without my father’s help. The confusion grew when I watched my younger siblings play in the mud while I struggled to keep up with chores that felt like adult responsibilities.

One evening, while sorting fabric for my grandmother’s creation of a quilt, I overheard her say, “You’re not a child anymore, even if you don’t want to admit it.” Her words stung, but they made sense. The reduction in my allowance for “wasting time” and the combination of new expectations—like helping with harvests—proved she was right. That night, I found an old thesis notebook in the attic, filled with my childhood scribbles. Flipping through it, I laughed at my childish drawings but also felt a strange pride. I wasn’t a kid who needed hand-holding anymore; I was someone who could contribute, even if clumsily.

Distractors: her lack of physical strength / her refusal to help with farmwork / her disappointment in her father’s teaching

Correct: it reminds her of her childhood while reinforcing her new self-image
Distractors: it proves she was always capable of adult tasks / it makes her feel nostalgic but regretful / it distracts her from her farmwork
Explanation: The notebook’s childish drawings contrast with her current role, helping her acknowledge her growth. It does not prove capability, evoke regret, or distract her—it serves as a reflective tool.

Topic: a personal essay on the biggest misconception people have about me

Text:
One of the biggest misconceptions people have about me is that I’m some kind of fearless adventurer. The truth is far less dramatic—and far more ordinary. When I was younger, I once borrowed a loan from my parents to buy a used motorcycle, convinced I could handle the expansion of my horizons. Instead, I spent a week stuck in a muddy cave near the coast after getting lost, my phone dead and my pride shattered. The whole ordeal was so hilarious in hindsight that I now laugh about it, but at the time, I was mortified. My parents never asked for an apology—they just reminded me that confidence isn’t the same as competence.

Another misunderstanding is that I’m a natural leader. In reality, I’m more comfortable in the background, observing and listening before speaking. People assume I’m assertive because I occasionally voice my opinions, but the truth is, I’m sufficiently introverted to overthink every word. I value unity in teams, but I’d rather facilitate discussions than dominate them.

Finally, some believe I’m anti-organic because I love technology. I once argued with a friend that a smartphone could track my garden’s soil moisture better than my "green thumb." She called me a hypocrite, but I just smiled—because the data proved her wrong. The misconception stems from the idea that caring about nature means rejecting innovation, which is absurd. After all, even the most traditional farmers now use apps to monitor crops.

Questions:
Q1: What does the author imply about their motorcycle adventure?
Correct: It was an embarrassing but ultimately harmless experience.
Distractors: It was a dangerous near-death experience / It taught them a valuable lesson about financial responsibility / It was a deliberate attempt to prove their bravery.
Explanation: The author describes getting lost in a cave as hilarious in hindsight and mentions being mortified at the time, suggesting embarrassment rather than danger or a life lesson. The text doesn’t mention financial responsibility or deliberate bravery—it’s framed as an impulsive, silly mistake.
---
Q2: Why does the author mention their argument about smartphones and gardening?
Correct: To challenge the stereotype that they dislike organic or natural methods.
Distractors: To show they are more technologically advanced than their friend / To prove that traditional farming is outdated / To explain why they avoid physical labor.
Explanation: The argument serves to counter the assumption that their love of technology means they reject organic practices. The text doesn’t compare technological skill with their friend or criticize traditional farming; it’s about bridging the gap between innovation and nature.
---
Q3: What does the author suggest about their leadership style?
Correct: They prefer to support others rather than take charge.
Distractors: They avoid conflict at all costs / They only speak when they’re certain they’re right / They believe leadership requires extroversion.
Explanation: The author describes themselves as introverted and more comfortable facilitating discussions than dominating them, implying a collaborative, supportive approach. The text doesn’t mention avoiding conflict entirely or needing certainty to speak—just careful consideration.
---
Q4: How does the author feel about the misconception that they’re fearless?
Correct: They find it amusing but frustrating.
Distractors: They are offended by it / They secretly enjoy the reputation / They think it’s a fair assessment of their personality.
Explanation: The author calls the adventure hilarious but also admits to being mortified at the time, suggesting a mix of amusement and irritation. There’s no indication they enjoy the reputation or believe it’s accurate.
---
Q5: What is the overall message of the essay?
Correct: People often misjudge others based on surface-level traits.
Distractors: Technology and nature are fundamentally incompatible / True leadership requires extroversion / Personal growth comes from facing physical challenges.
Explanation: The essay repeatedly highlights how others’ assumptions (about bravery, leadership, or preferences) don’t match reality. The text doesn’t claim technology and nature are incompatible, nor does it link leadership to extroversion or growth to physical challenges—those are tangential or incorrect interpretations.



Topic: a story about a time I had to navigate a complex bureaucratic system

Text:
Last year, I moved to a quiet suburb after years of living in an exotic city, hoping for a simpler life. But my first encounter with the local bureaucracy left me frustrated. I needed to declare my new address to update my driver’s license, but the process was far from straightforward. The office was outdated, and the staff seemed indifferent—no sympathy for someone unfamiliar with the system.

I arrived with my latest documents, only to be told I was missing a form I’d never heard of. When I asked for help, one clerk apparently assumed I was confused because of my accent, a negative assumption that felt like racism. I spent hours waiting, only to be sent to another department, then another, with no clear resolution in sight.

By the end, I realized I owed it to myself to learn the system. I returned the next day with a printed guide and a polite but firm attitude. This time, they processed my request efficiently. The experience taught me that bureaucracy, though frustrating, can be navigated—if you’re persistent and prepared.

Topic: a personal story about a time I felt like an outsider and how I coped

Text:
When I moved to a new city downtown, I felt like an outsider from day one. The neighborhood was predominantly white, and my racial background made me stand out in a way that felt uncomfortable. At first, I tried to alter my appearance—wearing neutral clothes, avoiding cultural accessories—but nothing seemed to rub off the slight tension I sensed. People would glance at me, and I couldn’t shake the feeling that I was being judged.

One evening, after a local café launched a new event, I attended alone. A coworker accused me of being too sensitive when I mentioned feeling excluded, saying my concerns weren’t relevant. That stung, but I realized the moral of the situation wasn’t about changing who I was—it was about finding my place. I started joining community groups, volunteering, and speaking up when I felt misunderstood. Over time, I learned that being an outsider wasn’t a flaw; it was just a phase. What mattered was how I responded. I stopped trying to fit in and instead focused on contributing in ways that felt appropriate to me.

Topic: a personal story about a conversation that fundamentally altered a key relationship

Text:

I had just submitted my thesis on cultural exchange and was feeling the weight of academic expectations. My friend Daniel, a fellow student, had always been my closest confidant, but our relationship had grown dull over time—buried under unspoken tensions and lingering offence from a past misunderstanding.

That day, I finally gathered the courage to address it. “I’ve been meaning to talk about what happened last summer,” I said, pushing the bill aside. “I never meant to hurt you.” Daniel listened quietly, his expression unreadable. Instead of defending himself, he admitted his own desire to restore our friendship but confessed he’d felt hurt by my silence. The honesty was beneficial—like a breath of fresh air after years of stagnation.

We spent hours discussing how to move forward, even joking about our failed attempts at placement in the same research group. By the end, the globe of resentment between us had shrunk to a pinprick. That conversation didn’t just mend our friendship; it taught me that vulnerability, not pride, is the key to lasting connections.

Topic: a story about a time I discovered a secret that changed my perception of my family

Text:
Last summer, while helping my senior uncle repair the old boiler in our basement, I noticed something unusual. The steam rising from the pipes wasn’t just from the heating system—it smelled faintly of herbs. Curious, I followed the scent to a hidden compartment behind a loose brick. Inside, I found jars of dried lavender, chamomile, and even a small notebook filled with handwritten recipes for herbal remedies.

At first, I assumed my uncle was just an eccentric hobbyist, but the more I read, the more I realized this was no casual interest. The notebook dated back decades, with notes on timing, protein-rich diets for recovery, and even a bet he had made with my grandmother about which remedy would work best for her arthritis. Moreover, the recipes were used extensively—some pages were dog-eared, and there were scribbled notes about who in the family had benefited from each one.

The most shocking discovery was a letter from my grandmother, tucked into the back. She wrote that my uncle had secretly provided assistance to neighbors in need for years, using these remedies, but had never wanted anyone to know. His tendency to stay quiet about his good deeds had always puzzled me, but now I understood. He wasn’t just a quiet man—he was a guardian of family secrets, ensuring no one would ever feel alone.

That night, I assured him I wouldn’t tell anyone, but the secret changed everything. I no longer saw him as just the strict, no-nonsense uncle who scolded me for eating too much sugar. He was a hero, quietly healing others while pretending it was nothing.

Questions:
Q1: What initially made the narrator suspicious about the steam in the basement?
Correct: Its unusual herbal scent
Distractors: The loud noise coming from the pipes / The fact that the steam was unusually cold / The unusual color of the steam
Explanation: The text states, "The steam rising from the pipes wasn’t just from the heating system—it smelled faintly of herbs." The other options are not mentioned: there is no reference to noise, temperature, or color of the steam.
---
Q2: Why did the narrator think the herbal remedies were more than just a hobby?
Correct: The notebook contained decades of detailed records and usage notes.
Distractors: The recipes were written in a foreign language. / The jars were labeled with expensive ingredients. / The remedies were only used by professionals.
Explanation: The text explains, "The notebook dated back decades, with notes on timing, protein-rich diets... Some pages were dog-eared, and there were scribbled notes about who in the family had benefited." None of the distractors are supported—there’s no mention of a foreign language, expensive ingredients, or professional use.
---
Q3: What did the grandmother’s letter reveal about the uncle’s secret activities?
Correct: He had been helping neighbors in secret for years.
Distractors: He had been experimenting with illegal substances. / He had been hiding a criminal past. / He had been selling the remedies for profit.
Explanation: The letter stated, "my uncle had secretly provided assistance to neighbors in need for years." The other options contradict the text, which describes his actions as altruistic, not criminal or profit-driven.

Distractors: They felt betrayed by his secrecy. / They decided to take over his herbal business. / They stopped respecting him because of his deception.
Explanation: The text says, "I no longer saw him as just the strict, no-nonsense uncle... He was a hero." The other options are unsupported—there’s no mention of betrayal, business plans, or loss of respect.

Distractors: The uncle assured the narrator that the remedies were safe. / The narrator assured the uncle they would help him with the remedies. / The uncle assured the narrator he would stop helping others secretly.
Explanation: The text states, "That night, I assured him I wouldn’t tell anyone." None of the other options are mentioned—there’s no discussion of safety, future collaboration, or the uncle’s plans to stop helping others.



Topic: a story about the most challenging conversation I ever had to initiate

Text:
The most challenging conversation I ever had to initiate was when I had to ask my boss for a raise after five years of working at the company. The idea of speaking up terrified me—I had always been the quiet type, preferring to let my work feed into my reputation rather than demand attention. But after months of taking on extra responsibilities, including leading a team through a difficult project that saved the company a tonne of money, I knew I deserved more.

The conversation was scheduled for a Monday morning, and I spent the weekend rehearsing in my head. My boss, Mr. Thompson, was charming but had a reputation for being blunt, so I braced myself for a sharp response. When I finally sat across from him, my hands trembled as I began: "I’ve been thinking about my contributions over the past year, and I believe my role has evolved significantly." His expression didn’t change, but I pressed on, explaining how my work had helped the company avoid a costly curve in expenses.

What followed was a tense silence. He leaned back, crossed his arms, and said, "You’ve done well, but raises aren’t handed out like candy." My heart sank—I had expected this. Then he added, "However, if you can show me how you’ll add even more value, we can revisit this in three months." Relief washed over me. He wasn’t dismissing me; he was giving me a chain of opportunities to prove myself further.

That conversation taught me that motivation to speak up is just the first step—assistance from others, even in small doses, can turn a harmful fear into a stepping stone. The date of our follow-up meeting became a reminder: persistence matters more than perfection.

Distractors: To imply he is genuinely kind and supportive / To indicate he is easily influenced / To show he is overly formal in conversations
Explanation: The narrator uses "charming" to contrast Thompson’s likable appearance with his blunt, potentially difficult personality. "Easily influenced" and "overly formal" are unsupported—Thompson’s demeanor is described as relaxed (leaning back, crossed arms) but not easily swayed.
---

Correct: A sudden, unexpected increase in financial losses.
Distractors: A gradual decline in profits / A planned budget adjustment / A minor oversight in accounting
Explanation: "Curve" here implies an abrupt change, not a gradual trend ("gradual decline"). The context suggests the project averted a significant financial hit ("saved the company a tonne of money"), so "sudden increase in losses" fits best. "Planned budget adjustment" and "minor oversight" contradict the severity implied by "costly."
---
Q3: How does the narrator’s description of rehearsing the conversation contribute to the story?
Correct: It emphasizes the psychological challenge of initiating the talk.
Distractors: It shows the narrator was unprepared for the meeting / It proves the narrator had no real motivation to ask for a raise / It suggests the narrator was overly confident in the outcome
Explanation: Rehearsing indicates anxiety and preparation, not lack of readiness ("unprepared"). The narrator clearly had motivation (five years of extra work), and the outcome was uncertain, so "overly confident" is wrong. The rehearsal highlights the mental barrier, not a lack of desire.
---
Q4: Why does the narrator mention the "chain of opportunities"?
Correct: To explain that the raise discussion led to a structured path for future growth.
Distractors: To criticize Thompson for setting unrealistic conditions / To suggest the narrator failed to secure immediate results / To imply Thompson was manipulating the process
Explanation: The "chain" metaphor shows a sequence of steps (prove value → revisit raise), not criticism or failure. "Unrealistic conditions" and "manipulation" are unsupported—Thompson’s response was pragmatic, not malicious.
---
Q5: What is the narrator’s main lesson from this experience?
