Distractors: Always demand immediate rewards for hard work / Avoid difficult conversations to prevent harm / Persistence is unnecessary if others don’t respond positively
Explanation: The narrator concludes that motivation to act (speaking up) and seeking assistance (follow-up plan) turned a feared moment into growth. "Immediate rewards" contradicts the three-month timeline. "Avoid difficult conversations" is the opposite of the lesson. "Persistence is unnecessary" is false—the text highlights persistence as key.



Topic: a story about inheriting a meaningful but mysterious object from a relative

Text:
When Uncle Richard passed away, he left me a small, curved wooden box with no lock—just a single key tucked inside an old letter. The box had belonged to his grandfather, a man who had shifted from poverty to victory in business, though he never spoke of how. Uncle Richard had kept it in silence, passing it down only when he felt his time was near.

I examined the box closely, running my fingers over its smooth surface. Inside, there was nothing but a faded photograph of a young man standing beside a ship—and a single, yellowed note. The handwriting was shaky, the words bitter: "They said I was foolish to trust the sea. But the sea gave me more than land ever could." I spent weeks trying to uncover its meaning, desperately searching for clues in Uncle Richard’s old journals. Gradually, I pieced together fragments: the ship in the photo was his grandfather’s first voyage, a journey that had saved the family from miserable poverty. Yet the note’s tone suggested a deeper sorrow—perhaps a betrayal or a lost love tied to that trip.

One evening, while sorting through Uncle Richard’s things, I found a finding hidden beneath the floorboards: a ship’s logbook with the same ship’s name. The final entry read, "The storm took her, but not before she gave us her last gift." I realised the box wasn’t just a relic—it was a puzzle, a story waiting to be told. Now, I’m determined to uncover the truth, even if it means sailing the same route my great-uncle once did.

Questions:

Distractors: To suggest it was damaged / To indicate it was heavy / To show it was old-fashioned

The focus is on its visual uniqueness, which hints at its significance.

Distractors: He moved from one country to another / He changed his career completely / He won a legal battle against someone
Explanation: The phrase implies a financial transformation—from struggling (poverty) to succeeding (victory). There’s no mention of relocation, career change, or legal disputes. The context of the ship and business aligns with economic success rather than personal or legal struggles.

Q3: Why does the narrator say the note’s tone was "bitter"?
Correct: It expressed resentment or regret about the past.
Distractors: It was written in a hurry / It contained harsh criticism of someone / It was difficult to read because of the old handwriting
Explanation: "Bitter" here describes the emotional tone of the note—suggesting unresolved anger or sadness. While the handwriting was shaky (implying difficulty), and the note might have been written quickly, the key detail is the emotional weight of the words, not their physical presentation or content. The note doesn’t explicitly criticise anyone, though the implication of betrayal exists.

Distractors: Search through old journals / Look for hidden clues in the house / Feel frustrated by the lack of answers

Correct: The ship’s final voyage provided the family with something valuable (the box).
Distractors: The storm destroyed the ship and everything on it / The ship’s captain betrayed the family / The "gift" was a physical object found in the storm’s wreckage
Explanation: The phrase suggests the ship’s last contribution to the family was the box (or its contents), despite being lost in a storm. It doesn’t imply total destruction (the box survived), betrayal (no mention of deceit), or a separate "gift" found later—the box itself is the inherited mystery. The logbook’s tone frames the ship’s end as bittersweet, with the box as a legacy.



Topic: a personal account of a journey I took to trace my family's history

Text:
When I decided to trace my family’s history, I knew it would be a moving experience—both emotionally and physically. My great-grandfather had fled from a small village in Eastern Europe, and his story had always fascinated me. I equipped myself with old letters, a scholarship from the local university, and a sense of curiosity that bordered on obsession.

The journey began in a bustling urban center, where I met a historian who warned me about the dangers of radiation in some abandoned archives. "Many records were stored in basements after the war," she said, her voice tinged with wisdom. I had been conscious of the risks but didn’t let them deter me. The first clue led me to a gang of local historians who had spent decades piecing together forgotten stories. Their knowledge was invaluable, though some details still hurt—like learning my ancestor had been accused of collaborating out of survival, not loyalty.

One aspect of the trip that surprised me was how much the landscape had changed. The village my great-grandfather described was now a shadow of its former self, swallowed by modern development. Yet, the oral histories I collected there were priceless. By the end, I realized the journey wasn’t just about facts—it was about understanding the layers of pain, resilience, and wisdom that shaped my family.

Distractors: to explain why the archives were difficult to access / to show that the historian was untrustworthy / to suggest that the records were already damaged
Explanation: The historian’s warning highlights the physical dangers (radiation) of accessing the archives, which the writer acknowledges but doesn’t let stop them. It doesn’t explain access difficulties (the archives were in a city, not necessarily sealed), nor does it imply the historian was untrustworthy (she was described as knowledgeable). The records weren’t described as pre-damaged—only that their storage was hazardous.

Distractors: They were criminals exploiting family secrets. / They were government employees controlling access. / They were amateur enthusiasts with unreliable information.

There’s no suggestion they were criminals, government-affiliated, or unreliable—they were described as having "invaluable" knowledge. The word gang is framed positively in this context.

Correct: It caused emotional pain but didn’t change their resolve.
Distractors: They were shocked and abandoned the research. / They believed the accusation was false. / They felt proud of their ancestor’s survival tactics.
Explanation: The writer admits the accusation "hurt" but continues the journey, showing emotional impact without giving up. There’s no mention of abandoning research, dismissing the accusation outright, or pride in collaboration—only acknowledgment of a painful truth.

Q4: What does the phrase "the journey wasn’t just about facts" suggest?

Distractors: The writer found the historical records incomplete. / The writer regretted focusing too much on logistics. / The writer realized the trip was a waste of time.
Explanation: The phrase contrasts facts (objective information) with layers of pain, resilience, and wisdom, implying the writer prioritized the human story over dry records. It doesn’t suggest the records were incomplete, that logistics were the issue, or that the trip was fruitless—only that the deeper meaning mattered more.

Distractors: Nostalgic / Hopeful / Indifferent

Nostalgic would require a focus on fond memories, which isn’t emphasized. Hopeful or indifferent contradict the emotional weight of the transformation described.



Topic: a story about a time I had to live with a difficult person and what it taught me

Text:
When I moved in with my uncle for a year, I never expected him to be such a puzzle. A retired professor with pure academic habits, he had little patience for small talk or casual routines. His expectations were high—silence after 10 PM, dishes washed immediately, and no guests without prior notice. At first, I resented his rigid rules, but I soon realised his ways weren’t arbitrary; they were the result of a full-time career spent opposing chaos.

One evening, after a heated argument over my messy kitchen, he handed me a book on time management and said, “Adjust or leave.” I laughed—his humorous bluntness caught me off guard—but deep down, I knew he was right. We struck a compromise: I’d keep the kitchen tidy, and he’d let me host one friend a week. The trading of small freedoms for responsibility taught me more than any lecture ever could.

Living with him wasn’t easy, but it forced me to grow. I learned that adequate communication—even with difficult people—requires effort. His stubbornness became my teacher, and his speculation that I’d fail turned into motivation to prove him wrong. By the end, I wasn’t just surviving; I was thriving.

Topic: a fantasy story about a librarian who discovers the books can rewrite themselves

Text:

For years, he had maintained a steady routine—organizing books, assisting readers, and ensuring every cell of the library’s vast collection remained intact. But one evening, while shelving a newly acquired volume of ancient poetry, Elias noticed something strange. The book’s pages seemed to shift on their own, as if rewritten by an invisible hand.

Curious, Elias traced his fingers over the words, only to find them changing before his eyes. The poem about love transformed into a tale of adventure, then into a interpretation of a forgotten prophecy. His heart raced with excitement and fear. Was this magic, or was he losing his mind? Determined to uncover the truth, Elias decided to elect a small group of trusted colleagues to accompany him in his investigation.

The following days brought more discoveries. Books on history rewrote themselves to reflect alternate timelines, while novels of fantasy evolved into optimistic visions of the future. Elias realized the library’s books were not just storing stories—they were alive, rewriting reality itself. Though the implications terrified him, Elias could not deny the wonder of it. Perhaps, he thought, the library was not just a place for books, but a gateway to endless possibilities.

Topic: an adventure story about a mapmaker charting a continent that reshapes itself constantly

Text:
Captain Elias Voss had spent years charting the shifting lands of Nexara, a continent where mountains rose overnight and rivers vanished by dawn. His latest expedition was no different—until the incident with the Whispering Cartographers, a gang of rival mapmakers who claimed his latest sketches were fraudulent. "Your lines don’t register the true shape of the land!" they sneered, brandishing a bullet-riddled map as "proof" of sabotage.

Elias scoffed. "Your work is hilarious—like tracing a storm!" he retorted, though privately, he wondered if the scandal might cost him his reputation. His first mate, Lira, urged him to consult the elders, but Elias refused, firmly convinced his methods were sound. Nevertheless, he decided to register his findings with the Guild before the next tremor reshaped the coastlines.

As they worked, Lira studied his notes closely, her brow furrowing. "The eastern delta isn’t where you marked it," she said. Elias grinned. "That’s because it moved while we slept." The land of Nexara was alive—and so was its greatest mystery.

Topic: a fantasy story where the main character realises that their world is an illusion

Text:
Lena had always believed her world was real—until the day she noticed the cracks. For years, she had followed the same routine, waking at dawn, working in the factory, and returning home to a small apartment where the walls never changed. The townsfolk spoke in random phrases, repeating the same jokes, the same complaints, as if their words were scripted. She had never questioned it—until now.

One evening, while folding laundry, Lena paused. The fabric in her hands felt too smooth, almost like paper. She looked up and saw the sky dominate the horizon, but the clouds moved in unnatural patterns, shifting too quickly, as if someone had pressed forward on a reel. A voice whispered in her mind: "You don’t have to accept this." The thought was hers, yet it felt foreign, as if an unseen agency had planted it.

The next morning, Lena entered the town square, where a lottery was being held—a weekly event where names were drawn for extra rations. She watched as the mayor called out winners, but the list seemed unacceptable to her. "Why these people?" she muttered. A child nearby giggled and said, "Because the script says so." Lena’s breath caught. Script? The word echoed in her mind, and suddenly, she understood: her world was a performance, a grand illusion, and she had just been given a choice—to step offstage or keep playing her part.

Questions:
Q1: What does the phrase "the script says so" reveal about Lena’s world?
Correct: The world operates on predetermined rules that its inhabitants cannot change.
Distractors: The townsfolk secretly control the illusion. / The lottery is rigged by an outside force. / The mayor writes the script for the townspeople.
Explanation: The child’s remark implies that events follow a fixed narrative ("the script"), meaning the world’s rules are unchangeable by its residents. There’s no evidence the townsfolk control the illusion or that an outside force rigs the lottery—the mayor’s role is passive. The idea that the mayor writes the script is contradicted by the child’s implication that the script exists independently of any single person.
---
Q2: Why does Lena’s discovery of the illusion feel "foreign"?
Correct: It challenges her long-held belief in the reality of her world.
Distractors: She realizes she has no control over her thoughts. / The illusion’s creators are manipulating her mind. / She suddenly remembers a past life outside this world.
Explanation: The "foreign" sensation stems from the contradiction between her lifelong certainty and the new realization that her world is artificial. There’s no suggestion her thoughts are controlled or that she remembers another life—only that the idea of an illusion arises unexpectedly. The "unseen agency" planting the thought doesn’t imply mind control but rather an external force revealing the truth.
---
Q3: How does the lottery scene contribute to Lena’s realization?
Correct: It highlights the arbitrary and repetitive nature of her world.
Distractors: It proves that the townsfolk are aware of the illusion. / It shows that Lena is the only one who can see the truth. / It demonstrates that the illusion is collapsing.
Explanation: The lottery’s randomness ("random" winners) and the child’s casual mention of "the script" underscore the world’s artificial, cyclical structure. The townsfolk aren’t aware of the illusion (they accept the lottery as normal), and Lena isn’t uniquely perceptive—she’s just the first to question it. There’s no evidence the illusion is "collapsing," only that Lena is noticing its flaws.
---
Q4: What does the word "agency" suggest about the source of Lena’s doubt?
Correct: An external, possibly supernatural force is influencing her awareness.
Distractors: Lena’s doubt comes from her own subconscious mind. / The townsfolk collectively decided to reveal the truth. / The factory where she works is the source of the illusion.
Explanation: The text describes the thought as planted by an "unseen agency," implying an outside entity (not Lena’s own mind or the townsfolk). There’s no link between the factory and the illusion’s mechanics, nor any suggestion the townsfolk orchestrated the revelation. The "agency" is vague but clearly external.
---
Q5: Why is Lena’s world described as an "illusion" rather than a dream?
Correct: It emphasizes the deliberate, constructed nature of her reality.
Distractors: Because dreams are personal and this world is shared. / Because illusions are easier to break than dreams. / Because the text mentions "script" and "performance."
Explanation: The term "illusion" (paired with "script" and "performance") suggests a carefully designed, collective deception, not just a personal dream. While shared reality is a valid point, the text’s focus on artificiality (e.g., "folding laundry like paper," "pressed forward on a reel") leans toward a constructed system rather than a psychological state. The idea that illusions are "easier to break" isn’t supported—Lena’s realization is sudden but not necessarily simpler than waking from a dream.



Topic: a fantasy story about a city built on the back of a slowly moving, colossal creature

Text:
The city of Eldermere was not built on land, but on the back of Vorthas, a colossal, slow-moving creature whose soul was said to be woven into the very stones of its home. For centuries, the people of Eldermere had occupied the creature’s broad, scaly hide, their homes and markets clinging to its ridges like barnacles to a whale. The relationship was one of mutual survival—Vorthas provided shelter and transport, while the city’s inhabitants offered warmth, food, and the occasional purchase of rare minerals from its back.

Yet, life in Eldermere was far from easy. The creature’s movements, though gentle, were unpredictable, and the city’s domestic disputes often took on a metaphorical weight—every argument felt like a tremor beneath one’s feet. The elders placed great emphasis on safety, issuing certificates to those who proved skilled in navigating the shifting terrain. A single misstep could mean a long, dangerous fall into the abyss below.

The greatest challenge, however, was not the physical danger but the psychological toll. Many citizens struggled with the knowledge that their home was, in essence, a living being. Some whispered that Vorthas was not merely a beast but a forgotten god, its slow, rhythmic breathing the pulse of the world. Whether true or not, the thought haunted them, making every decision—from where to build a new home to which path to follow—feel like a sacred trust.

Correct: It is one of interdependence, where both benefit from each other.
Distractors: It is purely parasitic, with Eldermere exploiting Vorthas / It is one of fear, as Vorthas actively hunts the city’s inhabitants / It is a temporary arrangement, as the city will soon abandon Vorthas.
Explanation: The text states that Vorthas provides shelter and transport while the city offers warmth, food, and trade, implying mutual benefit. The idea of exploitation or hunting is unsupported, and there is no mention of the city planning to leave. The relationship is described as long-standing and necessary, not temporary.
---
Q2: Why do the elders of Eldermere issue safety certificates?

Topic: a suspenseful story about a portal to a dimension where thoughts become reality

Text:
The old bookstore in the alley smelled of damp paper and something exotic—like ozone after a storm. Elias had always avoided it, but curiosity preceded fear that evening. The shopkeeper, a gaunt man with ink-stained fingers, had warned him about the comparative rarity of the tome he sought: "The Mind’s Labyrinth." Elias ignored the warning. Inside, the pages whispered as he turned them, until he found the passage about the portal hidden beneath the city’s abandoned subway. It described a place where thoughts materialized instantly—a risky contest with reality itself.

The first time Elias stepped through, he expected fireworks. Instead, the air turned thick, like syrup, and his shadow wormed across the wall, twisting into a grotesque figure. He tried to retreat, but the portal sealed shut. His mind raced: "I don’t want to be here." The words hung in the air—then a gust of wind materialized, knocking him to the ground. His elbow struck a jagged rock, and pain flared. "Stop hurting me," he gasped. The rock dissolved into mist, but the floor beneath him split open, revealing a chasm where his unfortunate thought—"I’m trapped"—had already taken shape.

A voice echoed: "You can’t accuse the dimension of your fears." Elias realized too late that every thought here was a command. His next one, "I need a way out," summoned a bridge of light—but it led deeper into the labyrinth. The shopkeeper’s warning flashed in his mind: "Some doors shouldn’t be opened."

Topic: an essay on the societal impact of the four-day work week

Text:
The idea of a four-day work week has gained significant attention in recent years, sparking debates about productivity, well-being, and societal change. While some argue that reducing work hours could harm trading economies, others believe it could improve nutrition, mental health, and work-life balance. A neutral study in Iceland found that employees working shorter hours were more satisfied and fulfilled their roles just as effectively. Critics, however, question whether businesses can attempt such reforms without financial strain.

One relevant example comes from Microsoft Japan, which tested a four-day week in 2019. Productivity surged by 40%, and employees reported better nutrition and energy levels. Yet, not all sectors can adopt this model easily—trading firms, for instance, may struggle due to global market demands. The signature success of such trials depends on flexible policies and employee cooperation.

Despite challenges, the movement persists. Some countries now beg for pilot programs, while others resist, fearing disruption. The subject remains complex: balancing economic needs with human well-being is no simple task.

In an era of globalization and constant mobility, the concept of home has become more fluid than ever. For many, home is no longer just a physical place but a golden ideal—a sanctuary where memories, traditions, and emotional bonds are preserved. Yet, as borders blur and people move across continents, the idea of home is being redefined.

Consider the early settlers who left their homelands in search of opportunity. Their journey was often driven by necessity, whether economic recession or war, but it also carried the hope of building something new. Today, migrants and expatriates face similar choices, though their reasons may be more varied—career growth, education, or simply the desire for change. The apparent freedom of movement, however, comes with a cost: the erosion of routine and the struggle to maintain a sense of belonging.

A house may provide shelter, but home is marked by intangible things—the scent of a mother’s cooking, the laughter of childhood friends, the quiet comfort of familiar streets. These are the markers that make a place feel like home. Yet, in a world where people are increasingly disconnected from their roots, even these markers can fade. The challenge, thus, is to find ways to carry home within us, wherever we go. Perhaps the true essence of home lies not in its physical location but in the relationships and experiences that shape our identity, considerably enriched by the diversity of global life.

Topic: an article exploring the ethics of digital legacies and social media profiles after death

Text:
In the digital age, the question of what happens to our online presence after death has become increasingly urgent. Many people broadly assume that their social media accounts will simply vanish, but the reality is far more complex. Families often struggle to decide whether to possess—or, more accurately, inherit—these digital legacies. Some argue that deleting accounts is the wise choice, ensuring no further emotional harm or misuse of private data. Others believe that preserving profiles can honor a loved one’s memory, acting as a digital shrine.

However, ethical dilemmas exceed simple sentimental value. For instance, should a mate of the deceased have access to private messages? Could someone exploit these accounts for financial gain or to spread harmful emission-like misinformation? The debate also touches on legal gray areas: Who has the right to overcome privacy settings? Some countries have introduced laws to address this, but enforcement remains inconsistent.

Critics warn that unchecked digital legacies can perpetuate grief in unhealthy ways, much like how toxic relationships linger after death. Others compare the issue to martial law—strict rules are needed to prevent chaos. The key, many experts suggest, is a reasonably balanced approach: respecting the deceased’s wishes while protecting the living from exploitation.

Distractors: Ensuring no one can hack into accounts / Preventing environmental damage from data servers / Avoiding conflicts between family members over passwords
Explanation: The text emphasizes ethical dilemmas (e.g., honoring memory vs. protecting privacy) and legal uncertainties, not hacking, environmental impact, or password disputes. The wise choice and martial-law-like rules imply a need for structured decisions, not just technical or interpersonal issues.

In a world where smartphones and social media dominate our attention, the value of solitude has become a fragment of a forgotten art. Even in an era where convenience often replaces depth, taking time alone allowed me to reflect, recharge, and reconnect with myself.

Not everyone embraces solitude, of course. Many see it as a luxury or a sign of weakness. But in reality, it’s a necessity—a chance to escape the operator-driven world of likes and shares. The media’s coverage of constant busyness often glorifies distraction, yet history’s greatest thinkers, from Einstein to Virginia Woolf, relied on solitude to innovate. Perhaps the modern world’s obsession with connection is masking a deeper hunger for meaning—and solitude is the quiet tool to find it.

Topic: an article about the paradox of choice - why more options often make us less happy

Text:
The modern world offers an overwhelming volume of choices—from job opportunities to streaming services, from smartphones to immigration policies. Yet, research suggests that more options often lead to less satisfaction. Psychologists call this the paradox of choice: the more we have, the harder it becomes to decide, and the less happy we feel with our final pick.

Consider the circuit of decision-making in a rural town versus a bustling city. In rural areas, people often have fewer options, but they make decisions quickly and confidently. In cities, where logo-branded products dominate shelves, consumers spend hours comparing brands, only to question their choices later. Studies show that people who resist the urge to overanalyze tend to be happier, while those who chop through endless possibilities often regret their decisions.

Take the affair of choosing a career. A decade ago, graduates had clear paths: law, medicine, or teaching. Today, fields like data science or renewable energy add complexity. The result? Many feel paralyzed, unable to commit. Even simple tasks, like picking a meal, become stressful when faced with 20 options instead of three.

The solution? Limiting choices can simplify life. Companies like Apple or IKEA thrive by offering curated selections, not overwhelming volume. Perhaps the key is not to resist choice entirely, but to accept that less can be more.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text suggest about people in rural areas compared to city dwellers?
Correct: They make decisions more confidently.
Distractors: They have fewer career opportunities / They struggle with decision fatigue / They rely more on brand logos
Explanation: The text states that rural residents "make decisions quickly and confidently" due to fewer options, contrasting them with city dwellers who overanalyze. The other options are incorrect: rural areas aren’t described as lacking career opportunities, nor do they struggle with decision fatigue (the opposite is true). The text mentions logo-branded products in cities, not rural areas.
---
Q2: Why does the text mention Apple and IKEA?
Correct: To illustrate how limiting choices can reduce stress.
Distractors: To criticize their business models / To argue that rural businesses are better / To explain why people prefer brand loyalty
Explanation: The text notes that these companies "thrive by offering curated selections," implying that fewer options simplify life. The other options are wrong: the text doesn’t criticize them, compare them to rural businesses, or discuss brand loyalty.
---
Q3: What does "chop through endless possibilities" imply about decision-making?
Correct: It leads to regret and dissatisfaction.
Distractors: It speeds up the process / It increases confidence / It makes people more creative
Explanation: The text links overanalyzing ("chop through") to later regret, not efficiency or creativity. The phrase suggests frustration, not confidence or speed.
---
Q4: How does the text define the paradox of choice?
Correct: More options can reduce happiness.
Distractors: Fewer options always lead to better decisions / People in cities make better choices / Overanalyzing helps avoid mistakes
Explanation: The text explicitly states that "more options often lead to less satisfaction." The other options contradict this: the paradox isn’t about cities making better choices or overanalyzing being beneficial.
---
Q5: What is the text’s main argument about modern decision-making?
Correct: Simplifying choices can improve well-being.
Distractors: Technology has made decisions easier / Traditional careers are disappearing / People should avoid all brand choices
Explanation: The conclusion advocates for "less can be more" and cites curated options (e.g., Apple/IKEA) as examples. The other options are unsupported: the text doesn’t claim technology simplifies decisions, nor does it focus on traditional careers or rejecting brands entirely.



Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of remote work

Text:
The rise of remote work has transformed how many professionals approach their careers, offering both opportunities and challenges. One of its greatest advantages is flexibility—employees can specialize in their tasks without the constraints of a 9-to-5 schedule, often improving productivity. For example, a graphic designer might work during late-night hours when creativity peaks, while a customer service agent can align shifts with peak delivery times. This adaptability also benefits those with family responsibilities or health conditions, allowing them to balance work and personal life more effectively.

However, remote work isn’t without drawbacks. Without physical coverage in an office, collaboration can suffer, as spontaneous discussions or brainstorming sessions require deliberate scheduling. Some argue that the lack of a shared workspace ruins team cohesion, making it harder to build relationships or receive immediate feedback. Additionally, self-discipline becomes crucial—without a supervisor’s cue, procrastination or distractions at home can motivate poor time management.

The origin of remote work traces back to early 20th-century experiments, but the digital revolution of the 1990s accelerated its adoption. Today, companies must weigh these factors carefully. While remote work offers freedom, it demands trust, clear communication, and structured accountability to ensure success. Ultimately, its effectiveness depends on the individual’s ability to thrive outside traditional office lanes, proving that the future of work is as much about where you work as how you work.

Distractors: It stifles creativity due to lack of supervision / It only benefits those in technical fields / It requires employees to work longer hours

The distractors are incorrect because the text does not claim remote work stifles creativity, limits it to technical fields, or forces longer hours—it highlights adaptability as a key benefit.

Distractors: It eliminates the need for scheduled meetings / It forces employees to work in isolation / It makes feedback impossible to give

The distractors are wrong because remote work still requires meetings, doesn’t force isolation (though it can enable it), and feedback is possible but less immediate.

Q3: How does the text describe the role of self-discipline in remote work?

Distractors: It is unnecessary for successful remote workers / It replaces the need for accountability / It is provided by supervisors remotely
Explanation: The text explicitly states that "self-discipline becomes crucial" to avoid procrastination and distractions, as there’s no supervisor’s cue to keep employees on track. The distractors contradict this: self-discipline isn’t unnecessary, it doesn’t replace accountability (which is still needed), and supervisors don’t provide it remotely.

Topic: a case study on the use of gamification in advertising

Text:
In the competitive world of advertising, brands are constantly searching for innovative ways to grab attention. One strategy gaining traction is gamification—the use of game elements like points, rewards, and challenges to engage audiences. A recent case study by AdTech Insights examined how a fitness app leveraged gamification to boost user retention by 40%. Instead of traditional ads, the app introduced a "Save the Planet" campaign, where users earned points for completing workouts, unlocking badges, and sharing progress on social media. The incentive was clear: the more active users became, the closer they got to a "victory"—a virtual trophy and a donation to environmental causes in their name.

Critics argue that gamification can be harmful if overused, turning engagement into a race that leaves users feeling pressured. However, the fitness app’s approach avoided this pitfall by focusing on feedback—real-time updates on progress and milestones—rather than punishing users for inactivity. Additionally, the campaign secured extra funding through sponsorships, proving that gamification could be both effective and sustainable.

Yet, not all gamified ads succeed. A fast-food chain’s "Starve the Competition" campaign bombed when users complained about misleading rewards. The lesson? Gamification must align with brand values and offer genuine savings—whether financial, health-related, or emotional—to enhance trust, not exploit it.

Questions:
Q1: What was the main goal of the fitness app’s "Save the Planet" campaign?
Correct: To encourage physical activity through rewards and social sharing
Distractors: To raise money for environmental causes / To compete with other fitness apps / To punish users who skipped workouts
Explanation: The text states the campaign rewarded users for workouts and sharing progress, linking activity to a "victory" (trophy + donation). While donations were part of the incentive, the primary goal was engagement, not fundraising. There’s no mention of competing with other apps or punishing users—only feedback and rewards.
---
Q2: Why did the fast-food chain’s "Starve the Competition" campaign fail?
Correct: Users found the rewards misleading.
Distractors: It lacked a clear incentive / It was too complex for customers / It didn’t align with the brand’s health-conscious image
Explanation: The text explicitly says the campaign "bombed" due to complaints about misleading rewards. Urban planners are increasingly turning to genuine innovation to address the challenges of modern cities. One of the most talked-about trends is the rise of "vertical cities," where skyscrapers double as self-sustaining ecosystems. Cities like Dubai have already established pilot projects where buildings incorporate green roofs, solar panels, and even vertical farms. Critics argue that such designs are too expensive, but proponents point to long-term savings in energy and infrastructure.

Another hypothesis gaining traction is the idea of "15-minute cities," where residents can access all essential services within a 15-minute walk or bike ride. Paris was the first to adjust its urban layout to this model, and the results have been impressed—traffic congestion dropped by 20% in some neighborhoods. However, implementing this package of changes requires political will, as seen in a recent bill introduced in New York to rezone areas for mixed-use development.

The future of urban planning may also involve rocket science. Companies like SpaceX are collaborating with architects to design habitats for Mars, but early prototypes are already being tested in Earth’s most extreme environments. While these ideas sound futuristic, experts believe they could inspire low-cost solutions for overcrowded cities. The signature of successful urban planning, they argue, lies in balancing ambition with practicality—whether in a high-rise or a neighborhood park.

Topic: a review of different architectural approaches to building in earthquake-prone zones

Text:
Building in earthquake-prone zones demands creativity and precision. Architects worldwide have developed diverse strategies to defend structures against seismic forces. One approach, known as base isolation, involves placing flexible pads between a building and its foundation. These pads absorb shockwaves, allowing the structure to sway rather than collapse. Engineers at a leading institute in Japan have devoted decades to perfecting this method, citing its success in reducing damage during tremors.

Another method, dampers, uses mechanical devices to counteract movement. These can be documented as highly effective in high-rise buildings, where wind and quakes pose dual threats. However, their cost often limits their use in less wealthy regions. Traditional techniques, such as reinforced concrete or timber framing, remain appropriate in rural areas, where resources are scarce but diversity in construction styles persists.

A recent enquiry by the International Federation of Structural Engineers revealed that no single solution fits all scenarios. While modern technologies excel in urban centers, communities must balance innovation with practicality. For instance, a village in Peru might possess centuries-old adobe homes that naturally resist quakes due to their lightweight design. The key lies in adapting solutions to local needs, ensuring safety without sacrificing cultural identity.

Explanation: The text states that base isolation uses flexible pads to absorb shockwaves, enabling structures to sway rather than collapse. The other options are unrelated: height, wind blocking, or added weight are not mentioned as goals of base isolation.

Distractors: They are ineffective against wind / They require constant maintenance / They are banned by local laws

Topic: a story about the experience of returning to work after a long-term absence and feeling like a stranger

Text:

Even her elementary notes from the last project felt outdated, as if she’d missed an entire era.

Her manager, Lisa, had warned her the transition wouldn’t be easy. “You’ll need to rebuild your observation skills,” Lisa said during their pre-return enquiry. Clara nodded, but the reality hit harder than expected. During a team lunch, she overheard a joke about “the old system” and realized she had no idea what they were talking about. Worse, her name wasn’t even on the agenda for the next two weeks—just a portion of her old responsibilities had been reassigned.

That evening, Clara reviewed her old emails. A failed prototype from three years ago had been archived, but the lessons from it still haunted her. She wondered if she’d ever catch up. The office felt like a foreign country, where every dot on the org chart represented a mystery she couldn’t decode.

Topic: an adventure story about finding a hidden image under a painting

Skeptical but curious, Clara decided to investigate efficiently during the exhibition’s completion phase, when fewer observers were around.

Using a UV light borrowed from a fellow contestant, she scanned the canvas. To her positive surprise, faint outlines emerged: a figure holding a lantern, its face obscured by shadow. But as she traced the shape with her fingers, the paint beneath her touch flaked away, revealing not just a face but a bear—a symbol of strength in medieval heraldry. The discovery was thrilling, yet Clara felt a pang of disappointment when she realized the painting’s true value lay not in its beauty but in its historical consumption of resources to conceal the secret.

By the time she finished, the round of judges had ended, and the painting was already being packed. Clara rushed to document her findings, but the observer who had spotted her earlier now demanded answers. “You’ve uncovered something extraordinary,” he said, his voice tense. “But who will believe it now?”

Questions:
Q1: Why does Clara decide to investigate the painting during the completion phase of the contest?
Correct: To minimize the number of observers and avoid drawing attention.
Distractors: To ensure she had enough time to analyze the painting thoroughly / Because the judges had already left the exhibition / To avoid competing with other contestants for the UV light
Explanation: The text states Clara chose the completion phase when “fewer observers were around,” implying she wanted privacy. The other options are incorrect: there’s no mention of thorough analysis, judges leaving, or competition for equipment.
---
Q2: What does Clara discover beneath the painting’s surface?
Correct: A hidden portrait of a bear holding a lantern.
Distractors: A medieval map of the region / A self-portrait of the artist / A symbol of wealth, like a golden coin
Explanation: The text explicitly describes a “bear—a symbol of strength in medieval heraldry,” holding a lantern. The other options (map, self-portrait, coin) are not mentioned.
---
Q3: How does Clara react to her discovery?
Correct: She feels a mix of excitement and disappointment over its historical significance.
Distractors: She immediately contacts the contest organizers to claim the prize / She destroys the painting to protect the secret / She hides the evidence out of fear
Explanation: The text notes her “positive surprise” at the discovery but also a “disappointment” tied to its historical consumption of resources. The other options contradict the text’s details (no contact, no destruction, no hiding).
---
Q4: Why is the observer tense when he speaks to Clara?
Correct: He realizes she has uncovered a historically significant secret.
Distractors: He suspects she cheated in the contest / He wants to steal her findings for himself / He is angry that she damaged the painting
Explanation: The observer says, “You’ve uncovered something extraordinary,” implying awe, not accusation. The text provides no evidence of cheating, theft, or damage.
---
Q5: What does the phrase “historical consumption” most likely refer to?
Correct: The resources (time, materials) used to conceal the hidden image.
Distractors: The painting’s role in ancient rituals / The way the image was eaten by time / The fact that the painting was consumed by fire
Explanation: The text links “historical consumption” to the “resources to conceal the secret,” not rituals, decay, or fire. The other options are implausible in context.



Topic: a critical analysis of the wellness industry

The wellness industry has exploded in recent years, promising everything from physical vitality to emotional balance. Yet, beneath the soothing lyrics of yoga retreats and meditation apps lies a more complex reality. Critics argue that many programs fail to deliver on their claims, often prioritizing profit over genuine well-being. For instance, a surgeon in New York’s affluent county recently opposed a luxury spa’s expansion, warning that its "detox" treatments lacked scientific backing. Meanwhile, the rise of "wellness zones" in urban areas has sparked debate: Are these spaces truly inclusive, or do they cater only to those who can afford them?

Even the most dedicated spectators—those who observe wellness trends without participating—question whether the industry adequately addresses mental health. A packed schedule of self-care workshops may feel fulfilling, but experts caution that true wellness requires more than fleeting trends. Some argue that the industry preserves inequality by framing well-being as a personal responsibility, ignoring systemic barriers like poverty or healthcare access. While the pursuit of balance is noble, the wellness boom risks becoming another commercialized self-help myth, detached from real-world struggles.

Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of minimalism in interior design

Text:
Minimalism in interior design has become a prime trend in recent years, appealing to those who value simplicity and functionality. One of its key advantages is the ability to create a calm and uncluttered space, which can significantly reduce stress. A minimalist workplace, for example, often enhances productivity by eliminating distractions. Additionally, minimalism encourages diversity in design choices, as it focuses on quality over quantity, allowing individuals to express their unique tastes through carefully selected pieces.

However, minimalism is not without its drawbacks. Some argue that overly minimalist spaces can feel confusing or sterile, lacking warmth and personality. Without enough decorative elements, a room may appear cold and unwelcoming. Another challenge is that minimalism requires discipline—establishing and maintaining such a space demands constant effort, especially in households with numerous possessions. Critics also point out that minimalism can be less entertaining for guests, as there are fewer objects to spark conversation or admiration.

Feedback from designers suggests that the key to successful minimalism lies in balance. While it offers clarity and elegance, it must not sacrifice comfort or creativity. The perception of minimalism varies widely; what one person finds serene, another might see as empty. Ultimately, the trend’s success depends on personal preference and the ability to adapt its principles without falling into extremes.

Distractors: It makes workplaces more entertaining for employees / It requires numerous decorations to be effective / It can lead to a confusing environment if not managed properly.
Explanation: The text states that minimalism in a workplace "often enhances productivity by eliminating distractions," making the correct answer clear. The other options are incorrect because the text does not claim minimalism makes workplaces more entertaining (it may even reduce entertainment), does not require numerous decorations (minimalism rejects excess), and does not suggest it inherently creates a confusing environment (though it can feel sterile, it is not described as confusing).

Explanation: The text explains that minimalist spaces can feel "sterile, lacking warmth and personality," which aligns with the idea of being confusing due to emptiness. The other options are incorrect: the text does not mention needing feedback from designers, establishing a theme is actually a goal of minimalism, and "too bombing" is nonsensical in this context (likely a typo for "overwhelming," but the text does not describe minimalism as overwhelming).

Many over-posters miss the magic of unplanned encounters because they’re too busy framing the perfect shot. Social media should enhance travel, not replace it.

Topic: a personal story about the moment I realized I was perpetuating a cycle I had sworn to break

Text:
The moment I realized I was perpetuating a cycle I had sworn to break came during a family gathering at my grandfather’s house. As the chairman of our small community, he had always been a figure of authority, but his sophisticated yet rigid approach to discipline had left scars. My father, impatient and quick to anger, had mirrored his methods—slapping my hand when I spoke out of turn, scolding me for minor mistakes. I had promised myself I would never do the same.

Then, at eight years old, I saw my little cousin, a cute toddler with wide eyes, reach for a plate of cookies. My grandfather’s voice echoed in my mind: "Children must learn obedience." Without thinking, I swatted her hand away. She burst into tears, and my father, watching from the corner, nodded approvingly. That night, I lay awake, studying psychology books my mother had left behind. The concept of breaking cycles hit me like a revelation. I had become the very thing I hated—the disciplinarian who crushed curiosity instead of nurturing it.

Subsequently, I made a conscious effort to change. When my own children misbehaved, I chose patience over punishment. But that day with my cousin? I had let fear of repeating history blind me. The troop of memories—my grandfather’s stern face, my father’s raised hand—had overpowered my better judgment. It wasn’t until years later, as a parent myself, that I understood: some cycles aren’t broken in a single moment. They’re unlearned, one small choice at a time.

Questions:
Q1: What does the narrator’s description of his grandfather as the chairman suggest about his influence?
Correct: His role gave him unquestioned authority in the family.
Distractors: He was respected for his kindness toward children / His position was symbolic and had no real power / He used his title to avoid personal responsibility.
Explanation: The grandfather’s title as chairman implies leadership and authority, which the narrator later describes as "rigid" and influential in shaping discipline. The text does not suggest kindness ("left scars"), nor does it imply his role was symbolic or that he avoided responsibility. His authority is central to the cycle of discipline.
---
Q2: Why does the narrator mention his father’s impatient nature?
Correct: To show how his father replicated his grandfather’s harsh methods.
Distractors: To explain why his father was often absent from family gatherings / To contrast his father’s behavior with his own later choices / To suggest his father was the primary cause of his trauma.
Explanation: The father’s impatience is tied to his use of physical discipline, mirroring the grandfather’s approach. The text does not mention absence, nor does it focus on the father as the sole cause—both men are part of the cycle. The contrast with the narrator’s later choices is implied but not the main point here.
---
Q3: How does the narrator’s reaction to his cousin’s tears differ from his grandfather’s?
Correct: He feels guilt and remorse, whereas his grandfather would have seen it as a lesson learned.
Distractors: He blames his cousin for provoking him / He immediately apologizes and offers her a cookie / He calls his father to intervene.
Explanation: The narrator’s tears and later reflection show guilt, contrasting with the grandfather’s likely approval of discipline. The text does not suggest blaming the cousin, an immediate apology, or involving his father—those actions would contradict the cycle he’s trying to break.
---
Q4: What does the narrator mean by "The troop of memories"?
Correct: The collective weight of his family’s disciplinary history overwhelming him.
Distractors: The literal presence of soldiers at the gathering / His memories of playing with toy soldiers as a child / The idea that his memories were organized like a military unit.
Explanation: "Troop" is metaphorical here, representing the oppressive influence of past generations’ behavior. There is no mention of soldiers, toys, or military organization in the text—only the psychological burden of inherited patterns.
---
Q5: What is the narrator’s final realization about breaking cycles?
Correct: It requires sustained effort, not just a single moment of awareness.
Distractors: It is impossible to fully escape one’s family’s influence / He had successfully broken the cycle years earlier / The cycle can only be broken by punishing children more severely.
Explanation: The narrator admits that unlearning cycles is a gradual process ("one small choice at a time"), rejecting the idea of instant change. The text does not claim impossibility, early success, or harsher punishment as solutions—those contradict his emphasis on patience and growth.



Topic: a story about mediating a conflict between two people I care about deeply

Text:
Last week, I found myself in the middle of a heated argument between my two closest friends, Mark and Lisa. Mark, a conservative lawyer, had accused Lisa, a passionate nursing student, of being reckless after she canceled their group project at the last minute. Lisa, visibly upset, claimed Mark had ignored her ethical concerns about the project’s impact on vulnerable creatures in a lab study. When I arrived, Lisa’s elbow was clenched, and Mark’s face was flushed with shock—neither seemed willing to listen.

I knew I had to act. Pulling out my old textbook on conflict resolution (a compulsory course I’d taken years ago), I reminded them that their regard for each other shouldn’t be unconscious of the bigger picture. “You’re both brilliant,” I said, “but this isn’t about winning. It’s about how you treat each other.” Mark sighed, while Lisa softened slightly. By the end, they agreed to revisit the project with clearer guidelines—no more assumptions, just collaboration.

Topic: an essay on whether smartphones should be banned in schools

Text:
The debate over whether smartphones should be banned in schools has intensified in recent years. While some argue that these devices distract students, others claim they are essential tools for learning. However, the risks of smartphones in educational settings often outweigh their benefits.

One major concern is the way smartphones can transmit harmful content. Social media platforms, for instance, may insert misleading information or cyberbullying messages into students’ feeds, creating an illusion of safety. Schools cannot always monitor every interaction, leaving students vulnerable. Additionally, the constant notifications and games can disrupt focus, making it difficult for students to engage in class discussions or complete assignments.

Some schools have tried to regulate smartphone use by designating "phone-free" zones, but enforcement remains inconsistent. A study by an independent agency found that even when students are allowed to use phones during breaks, they often crash into other students or ignore teachers’ instructions. The circumstance of a student’s phone ringing during a test or a presentation can also create unnecessary stress.

On the other hand, proponents of smartphone use argue that these devices can enhance education. Corporations like Apple and Google have developed apps that help students research topics, collaborate on projects, or even access insurance for school-related expenses. However, these benefits are often overshadowed by the risks. For example, a student might use their phone to chair a group discussion one moment and then get distracted by a grocery delivery app the next.

Ultimately, the decision to ban smartphones should consider both the potential advantages and the dangers. While technology can be a powerful learning tool, the uncontrolled use of smartphones in schools creates more problems than solutions.

Q1: What does the author suggest about the role of social media in schools?
Correct: It can transmit harmful content that creates an illusion of safety.
Distractors: It helps students stay connected with their families / It is strictly monitored by teachers / It improves students’ research skills
Explanation: The text states that social media can insert misleading information or cyberbullying, creating an illusion of safety. The other options are incorrect because the text does not mention family connections, teacher monitoring, or research improvements as primary concerns.
---
Q2: Why does the author mention the study by an independent agency?
Correct: To show that smartphones cause disruptions even during breaks.
Distractors: To prove that students use phones only for gaming / To argue that teachers should allow phone use / To explain how phones help with school projects
Explanation: The study highlights that students crash into others or ignore instructions when using phones during breaks. The other options are incorrect because the text does not focus on gaming, teacher approval, or project assistance.
---
Q3: What does the author imply about the word "chair" in the context of smartphone use?
Correct: It is used metaphorically to describe leading a discussion before getting distracted.
Distractors: It refers to a physical chair in the classroom / It means the phone is used as a substitute for a teacher / It suggests students should sit down while using phones
Explanation: The author uses "chair" figuratively to mean taking the lead in a discussion, only to be distracted by other apps (like a grocery delivery app). The other options misinterpret the word’s meaning.
---
Q4: According to the text, what is one circumstance where a student’s phone use is particularly problematic?
Correct: When it rings during a test or presentation.
Distractors: When it is used to take notes in class / When it helps students collaborate on group work / When it is turned off during recess
Explanation: The text explicitly states that phones ringing during tests or presentations create stress. The other options describe positive or neutral uses, not problems.

Distractors: Smartphones should be fully banned without exception / The advantages clearly surpass the dangers / Schools should rely on corporations to regulate phone use
Explanation: The author argues that while smartphones have some educational uses, their uncontrolled presence in schools leads to more harm than good. The other options are extreme or unsupported by the text.



Topic: a story about a friendship that ended not with a fight but with a slow, mutual drifting apart

Text:
The flame of Jake and Mira’s friendship had burned bright during their university years, fueled by shared regional traditions, late-night exploration of the city, and a consistent habit of meeting every weekend. They were inseparable—until they weren’t.

After graduation, their paths diverged literally and metaphorically. Jake moved to a new city for work, while Mira stayed close to family. Their conversations became shorter, replies delayed, and plans canceled without explanation. It wasn’t a fight; it was a slow, mutual drifting apart, like two ships passing in the night. Even when they encountered each other at a mutual friend’s host party, their exchanges were polite but hollow, lacking the warmth of old times. Mira once leaned in to share a joke, only to realize Jake was already turning away, his elbow resting on the table as if marking the distance between them.

One evening, Mira scrolled through old photos and paused at a selfie of them laughing under a streetlamp. The memory stung—not of anger, but of quiet regret. They had advanced in life, but not together. Some friendships fade like that, leaving no trace of conflict, just the echo of what once was.

Topic: a personal story about the moment I realized I was jealous of a friend's success

Text:
The moment I realized I was jealous of a friend’s success was as subtle as a infection—slow to spread but impossible to ignore once it took hold. It happened at a universal gathering of colleagues, where Jake, my equivalent in years and experience, announced he’d been promoted to team lead. His smile was effortless, his status now undeniably higher, and for the first time, I felt a sharp, unfamiliar nerve of resentment twist in my chest.

I’d always prided myself on fairness, but watching Jake’s participation in the celebration—laughing with the boss, receiving pats on the back—made me question my own scheme of self-worth. Was my success measured by the same universal standards, or had I been outbeat by someone who played the game better? The thought gnawed at me like an evil whisper, especially when I imagined the shore of my own stagnation, where I’d been drifting for months.

That evening, I confessed my turmoil to my partner. “It’s not just about the promotion,” I admitted. “It’s the way it feels—like I’ve been left behind.” Their response was simple: “Jealousy isn’t the problem. The problem is you’ve been comparing your behind-the-scenes work to his highlight reel.” It stung, but it was true. Jake’s rise wasn’t a universal truth of my failure; it was a reminder that success isn’t a zero-sum scheme.

Questions:
Q1: What does the narrator compare jealousy to in the opening sentence?
Correct: An infection
Distractors: A storm / A shadow / A wound
Explanation: The narrator describes jealousy as spreading “slow to spread but impossible to ignore,” mirroring how an infection works. A storm implies suddenness, a shadow suggests obscurity, and a wound implies pain rather than gradual corruption. The text explicitly uses “infection”.
---
Q2: Why does the narrator feel resentment toward Jake?
Correct: Because Jake’s promotion elevated his status in a way that made the narrator question their own worth.
Distractors: Because Jake lied about his achievements / Because the narrator was excluded from the celebration / Because Jake’s success was unfairly awarded
Explanation: The text states the narrator’s resentment stems from Jake’s “status” and the “highlight reel” contrast, not exclusion or accusations of lying. The promotion itself isn’t called “unfairly awarded”—it’s the comparison that causes the emotion.
---
Q3: What does the phrase “outbeat” imply about the narrator’s perception of Jake?
Correct: That Jake surpassed the narrator in some competitive or professional aspect.
Distractors: That Jake physically defeated the narrator / That Jake abandoned a shared project / That Jake’s success was temporary
Explanation: “Outbeat” here is used metaphorically to mean “outdone” or “outperformed”, not literal violence or abandonment. The context of promotions and status supports the competitive implication. The text doesn’t suggest Jake’s success was “temporary”.
---
Q4: What does the “shore” symbolize in the text?
Correct: A metaphor for stagnation or being stuck in one’s career.
Distractors: A literal place where the narrator lives / A boundary between success and failure / A safe haven from competition
Explanation: The “shore” is described as where the narrator had been “drifting”, symbolizing lack of progress. It’s not a literal location, nor does it represent a clear “boundary” (that would be more abstract). A “safe haven” contradicts the negative connotation of “stagnation”.

Topic: a story about how an unusual compromise resolved a conflict

Text:
The small village of Eldermere had long been divided between the old and the young. The elders, wise but stubborn, refused to embrace technological change, clinging to their traditional ways. Meanwhile, the younger generation, eager for progress, saw the village’s outdated systems as a burden. Tensions rose when the village council proposed replacing the old water pumps with modern ones—an idea the elders suspected was just a way to dominate them. Protests turned heated, and the village risked splitting apart.

One evening, as the debate reached its peak, an unexpected compromise emerged. The elders, though reluctant, agreed to keep one of the old pumps operational—but only if the younger generation reinforced it with a technological backup system. The opening for dialogue came when a young engineer, instead of arguing, suggested a hybrid solution: the old pump would remain as a backup, while the new system would handle daily needs. The elders, seeing the practicality, softened. The young people, realizing the elders’ concerns weren’t just spite, but fear of losing control, accepted the compromise. Within weeks, the village installed both systems, and the conflict dissolved. The elders gained security, the young gained efficiency—and the village thrived. The lesson? Even the deepest divides can heal when both sides listen beyond their first instincts.

Distractors: They believed the young people were trying to replace them in leadership / They thought the pumps were unsafe / They were unconscious of the benefits of modernization
Explanation: The text states the elders "refused to embrace technological change" and "suspected" the young wanted to "dominate" them, implying a fear of losing autonomy. The distractors are incorrect because the text does not mention leadership replacement, safety concerns, or the elders being unaware of benefits—only their resistance to change and fear of domination.

Explanation: The engineer’s suggestion to keep the old pump as a backup while using the new system for daily needs directly resolved the conflict by balancing tradition and progress. The distractors are wrong because the elders did not fully adopt the new system, the engineer did not ignore objections, and there’s no mention of him seeking authority.

Correct: They realized the young people’s intentions weren’t driven by spite.
Distractors: They admitted they had been unconscious of the village’s needs / They decided to dominate the younger generation instead / They agreed only because the new system was more efficient
Explanation: The text notes the elders "realized the young people’s concerns weren’t just spite," meaning they recognized the younger generation’s motives were constructive. The distractors are incorrect because the text doesn’t mention the elders being unaware of needs, shifting to domination, or agreeing solely for efficiency—only that they saw the compromise as fair.

Explanation: The phrase implies that the compromise was born from a shift in perspective, not surrender or premeditation. The distractors are wrong because the elders didn’t "give in," the young didn’t plan it, and the resolution required dialogue, not avoidance.

Distractors: Compromise always requires one side to sacrifice completely / Technological advancements are the only way to modernize / Conflicts are best resolved by ignoring traditional values
Explanation: The story highlights that the elders’ fear and the young’s impatience were addressed through mutual understanding, not sacrifice or rejection of tradition. The distractors are incorrect because compromise here was balanced, technology was only part of the solution, and traditional values were preserved, not ignored.

Today, EcoPuro employs over 150 people, many from the same village where the factory once stood. Their products, now sold globally, have reduced plastic waste by 40% in Maracaibo alone. López’s story is a reminder that innovation often begins with resilience, and that progress is possible wherever there is determination.

Topic: an essay on whether zoos should be banned

Text:
The debate over whether zoos should be banned is a complex subject that involves ethical, psychological, and practical considerations. While some argue that zoos provide a secure environment for endangered species, others claim that animals suffer from confinement, leading to psychological distress. For example, studies show that animals like elephants or big cats often exhibit repetitive behaviors, such as pacing, when kept in small enclosures—a clear sign of stress.

Proponents of zoos highlight their role in conservation, particularly for species on the brink of extinction. Skilled veterinarians and researchers work in zoos to improve animal nutrition, breeding programs, and even reintroduce species into the wild. However, critics argue that these efforts could be more effective if focused on protecting habitats overseas rather than relying on captivity. Some also question whether zoos truly prioritize animal welfare or prioritize profit, especially when exotic animals are displayed as monsters to attract visitors.

Another perspective is that zoos can motivate public interest in wildlife conservation. Visitors, especially children, often develop a connection with animals they see up close, which may inspire future support for environmental causes. Yet, this argument is weakened if the animals’ well-being is compromised. For instance, a mosque in a nearby city recently hosted an exhibition on ethical wildlife tourism, emphasizing that true conservation requires respect for animals’ natural behaviors.

Ultimately, the decision to ban zoos depends on how we evaluate their benefits against their ethical costs. While some zoos operate with high standards, others fail to meet basic welfare requirements. A balanced approach—supporting only well-regulated facilities while pushing for stronger global conservation efforts—may be the most responsible solution.

Distractors: Zoos are too expensive to maintain / Animals in zoos are not well-fed / Zoos only benefit wealthy countries.
Explanation: The text explicitly mentions that animals like elephants and big cats show signs of stress (e.g., pacing) due to confinement, linking this to psychological distress. The other options are not supported: cost is not the focus, nutrition is discussed but not as the primary concern, and there’s no claim that zoos only benefit wealthy nations.

Explanation: The text states that zoos use breeding programs and veterinary care to support endangered species, and it mentions protecting habitats overseas as an alternative. The other options are incorrect: zoos do not release animals freely, they do not teach hunting, and they do not replace wild reserves.

Correct: Some prioritize profit over animal welfare.
Distractors: They only display dangerous animals / They charge too much for entry / They fail to educate visitors properly.
Explanation: The text criticizes zoos that treat animals as monsters to attract visitors, implying profit motives over welfare. The other options are not mentioned: the text does not specify entry prices, nor does it claim zoos fail to educate (the mosque exhibition suggests public interest is possible).

Correct: It emphasizes respect for animals’ natural behaviors.
Distractors: It encourages people to visit more zoos / It argues that all zoos should be banned / It focuses on religious perspectives on animals.

Topic: an essay on how virtual reality might change education in the future

Text:

While some argue that traditional classrooms will never disappear, the evident advantages of VR suggest otherwise. Imagine students exploring ancient Rome without leaving their desks or dissecting a virtual frog without the risk of theft or damage to real specimens. One major concern is whether VR can replace examinations as we know them. Additionally, VR could reduce the exception of students who struggle with traditional teaching methods, offering personalized learning paths.

Art education, for instance, could transform entirely. Students might collaborate on digital artwork in shared virtual studios, receiving instant feedback from teachers or peers in an association that mimics real-world creativity. While challenges remain—such as cost and accessibility—VR’s potential to revolutionize education is undeniable. The question is not if it will change learning, but how soon and how deeply.

The question is no longer if we act, but how we unite these forces to turn the tide.

Topic: an essay on the psychology of risk-taking: why some people seek danger and others avoid it

Text:
The psychology of risk-taking reveals fascinating differences between individuals. Some people thrive on adrenaline, seeking danger like extreme sports or reckless driving, while others avoid it at all costs. Research suggests that personality traits play a key role—those who are naturally bold may view risks as exciting challenges, whereas cautious individuals see them as threats.

One angle to consider is the role of past experiences. A person who survived a near-miss accident might develop a loyal attachment to safety, while an opponent of risk-taking could be discouraged by fear of failure. For example, someone with a physical disability might avoid high-risk activities due to practical concerns, yet still crave adventure in controlled settings.

Neuroscientists also study how the brain processes risk. The completion of a thrilling task releases dopamine, reinforcing risky behavior. Conversely, those who fear failure may edit their perceptions, magnifying potential dangers. Even small factors, like a broken wrist or social recognition, can influence decisions—some take risks for validation, others to prove their resilience.

Ultimately, risk-taking is a spectrum. Understanding these motivations helps explain why some embrace danger while others prioritize security.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text suggest about the relationship between personality and risk-taking?
Correct: Bold individuals may see risks as exciting challenges.
Distractors: Risk-taking is solely determined by past accidents / Only cautious people enjoy adrenaline / Personality has no influence on risk behavior.
Explanation: The text states that "personality traits play a key role" and that "those who are naturally bold may view risks as exciting challenges." The other options are incorrect because the text does not claim risk-taking is solely determined by accidents, that only cautious people enjoy adrenaline (the opposite is implied), or that personality has no influence.
---
Q2: How might a person with a physical disability approach risk-taking, according to the text?
Correct: They might avoid high-risk activities but still seek adventure in controlled settings.
Distractors: They would never take any risks / They would be more reckless than others / Their disability would make them indifferent to danger.
Explanation: The text explains that someone with a disability "might avoid high-risk activities due to practical concerns, yet still crave adventure in controlled settings." The other options contradict this—avoiding risks entirely, being reckless, or indifference are not supported.
---
Q3: What role does dopamine play in risk-taking behavior, as described in the text?
Correct: It reinforces risky behavior by making it feel rewarding.
Distractors: It discourages people from taking risks / It only affects those with disabilities / It has no connection to risk-taking.
Explanation: The text states that the completion of a thrilling task "releases dopamine, reinforcing risky behavior." The other options are incorrect because dopamine is described as encouraging risk, not discouraging it, and it is not limited to those with disabilities or unrelated to risk-taking.

Topic: an essay discussing the ethics of using animals in medical research

Text:
The debate over animal testing in medical research is complex, involving moral, scientific, and practical aspects. While some argue that animals share a flavour of human biology, making them useful models, others question whether their suffering justifies progress. Critics highlight the poor housing conditions in many labs, where animals often live in cramped, sterile environments—far from the independence they might experience in nature.

Proponents of animal testing point to its brief but critical role in developing life-saving drugs. For example, vaccines for diseases like polio were first tested on primates before human trials. However, ethical referees—such as philosophers and veterinarians—argue that alternatives, like computer simulations or cell cultures, should be prioritised. Public opinion also plays a role; protests and parades demanding an end to animal testing have grown, pressuring governments to reconsider fare policies that fund such research.

Yet, the issue remains contentious. While regulations aim to minimise animal distress, loopholes persist. Some labs still treat animals as disposable, raising comparisons to historical abuses, like overcrowded jails for prisoners. The core question lingers: Can science advance without exploiting vulnerable beings, or is compromise the only path forward?

Topic: an article about the placebo effect

Text:
The placebo effect has long fascinated scientists and doctors, proving that the mind can play a powerful role in healing. Studies have demonstrated that patients given sugar pills—believing them to be real medicine—often experience real improvements in symptoms. This phenomenon challenges our understanding of medicine, suggesting that belief and expectation alone can trigger the body’s natural healing processes.

Researchers have tried to establish why placebos work. Some theories focus on the brain’s release of endorphins, while others highlight the importance of unity between patient and doctor. A famous experiment involved patients with chronic pain climbing a vertical ladder while receiving placebos. Many reported reduced discomfort, littered with anecdotes of sudden relief. Critics argue that the effect is purely psychological, but skeptics often overlook how deeply the soul can influence physical well-being.

One study even explored whether a simple password shared between doctor and patient could enhance the placebo response. Participants who knew their "code word" showed greater improvement than those who didn’t, reinforcing the idea that trust and communication wage a silent battle against illness. While placebos aren’t a cure, they remind us that healing isn’t just about pills—it’s about connection, belief, and the stories we tell ourselves.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text suggest about the relationship between belief and healing?
Correct: Belief alone can trigger the body’s natural healing processes.
Distractors: Belief only works if combined with actual medication / Belief has no scientific basis / Belief is less effective than traditional medicine.
Explanation: The text states that patients given placebos (sugar pills) often experience real improvements, proving belief can heal. The other options contradict the evidence: the text doesn’t claim placebos need medication, dismisses belief as ineffective, or suggests it’s less powerful than traditional medicine.
---
Q2: Why did researchers use a vertical ladder in their experiment?
Correct: To test how placebos affect physical pain during a challenging task.
Distractors: To measure how quickly patients could climb / To compare results with horizontal movement / To study the psychological impact of height.
Explanation: The ladder was used to induce pain while patients took placebos, allowing researchers to observe symptom relief. The focus wasn’t on speed, horizontal movement, or just psychology—it was about demonstrating placebo effects on physical discomfort.
---
Q3: How does the text describe the role of trust in placebo effectiveness?
Correct: Trust between doctor and patient enhances the placebo response.
Distractors: Trust is irrelevant to placebo effects / Trust only works if patients know a password / Trust replaces the need for medication.
Explanation: The study with the password shows that shared trust (even symbolic, like a code word) boosts placebo success. The text doesn’t say trust is useless, that passwords are required, or that trust eliminates medication—just that it strengthens the effect.
---
Q4: What does the phrase "the soul can influence physical well-being" imply?
Correct: Emotional and psychological factors affect healing.
Distractors: Healing depends solely on spiritual practices / The soul has a direct, measurable impact on the body / Physical health is unrelated to mental states.
Explanation: The text links the soul (metaphorically, emotional/psychological states) to healing, not literal spirituality or direct biological control. It rejects the idea that mental and physical health are separate.
---
Q5: Which word best describes the overall message of the paragraph about the password study?
Correct: Connection.
Distractors: Deception / Randomness / Authority.
Explanation: The password symbolizes trust and unity between patient and doctor, reinforcing that healing thrives on connection. The other options don’t fit: deception isn’t mentioned, randomness is irrelevant, and authority isn’t the focus—it’s about shared understanding.
