Topic: a case study on a country that recovered successfully from a major financial crisis

Text:

The country had inherited a fragile banking system from decades of rapid growth, where speculative bubbles in real estate and stock markets had inflated without proper regulation. When the Thai baht collapsed, foreign creditors withdrew their funds, leaving the economy in ruins. Yet, Thailand’s recovery became a model of resilience.

One key factor was the government’s focus on restructuring the financial sector. Scholars later praised its capacity to implement strict reforms, including recapitalizing banks and enforcing corporate debt restructuring. The distribution of aid was also critical—small businesses, which employed the majority of the workforce, received targeted support to prevent mass unemployment.

Infrastructure played a vital role. While construction projects had previously been driven by short-term gains, the post-crisis era prioritized sustainable development. Renewable energy investments reduced emission levels, aligning with global environmental preferences. By 2005, Thailand’s GDP growth rebounded, proving that disciplined economic policies could outweigh past excesses.

Topic: an article about neurolinguistics

Text:
Neurolinguistics is the scientific study of how the brain processes language, revealing fascinating connections between speech and cognition. One surprising discovery is that language consumption—how we absorb and interpret words—is far more dependent on organic brain structures than previously thought. For example, damage to specific regions can impair speech production, while others affect comprehension, proving that language is not a single, uniform skill but a multiple system.

Researchers have also explored how language shapes memory. A memorable phrase or song, like a choir performance, can trigger vivid recall due to the brain’s emotional and rhythmic engagement. Conversely, nasty or aggressive language may alter neural pathways, potentially hindering conservation of positive social interactions. Studies even suggest that exposure to certain sounds, such as radiation-like frequencies (e.g., white noise), can temporarily disrupt language recovery in stroke patients, delaying rehabilitation.

Yet, the brain’s plasticity offers hope. With targeted therapy, many patients can recover lost linguistic functions, demonstrating that while language is complex, it remains adaptable. Understanding these mechanisms could revolutionize education, therapy, and even artificial intelligence, bridging gaps between biology and technology.

Distractors: Language is primarily controlled by a single brain area. / The brain’s organic structure has no impact on language. / Language recovery is impossible after brain damage.
Explanation: The text states that language is a multiple system with dependent organic structures, meaning different regions handle different aspects. The distractors contradict this: a single area is not mentioned, organic structure is crucial, and recovery is possible with therapy.
---
Q2: How does the text explain the role of memorable language in memory?

Distractors: Memorable language always improves cognitive function. / Only written language affects memory. / Memorable language weakens neural pathways.
Explanation: The text links memorable phrases/songs to emotional or rhythmic triggers (e.g., choir) boosting recall. The distractors are too absolute ("always"), incorrect ("only written"), or contradict the idea of conservation of positive interactions.

Q3: What effect does aggressive language have on the brain, according to the text?

Distractors: It has no measurable impact. / It enhances language recovery. / It strengthens memory retention.
Explanation: The text describes nasty language as potentially disrupting neural pathways, not helping recovery or memory. The other options ignore the negative framing.

Q4: Why might white noise (e.g., radiation-like frequencies) delay language recovery in stroke patients?

While some studies show wage increases can be equivalent to productivity gains, others highlight the risk of inflation or reduced hiring. The criticism often centers on whether policymakers should urge gradual adjustments or implement sweeping changes.

Topic: an article about inventions predicted by science fiction writers

Text:
Science fiction has long inspired real-world inventions, and lately, the gap between fiction and reality has narrowed dramatically. One famous example is the virtual reality headset, first imagined in novels like Snow Crash (1992) by Neal Stephenson. Today, companies like Meta and Sony have turned this plot device into a mainstream technology, allowing users to interact with digital worlds as if they were real. Another prediction came from The Lawnmower Man (1995), which depicted a future where people could delete their memories or alter their consciousness—ideas now explored in experimental neuroscience.

Less futuristic but equally fascinating is the concept of remote work, popularized by stories like The Moon Is a Harsh Mistress (1966). While the prospect of working from anywhere was once science fiction, the pandemic forced a rush toward this reality. Now, many professionals manage their careers from home, blurring the lines between personal and professional life—much like the plain but revolutionary idea of a "paperless office" from 2001: A Space Odyssey.

Even real estate has been reimagined. In Ready Player One (2011), people abandoned physical estates for virtual ones, trading mortgages for digital property. Today, platforms like Decentraland allow users to buy virtual land, proving that fiction often anticipates economic shifts. The key takeaway? Science fiction doesn’t just entertain—it plants seeds for innovation, challenging us to ask: What will tomorrow’s writers invent today?

Distractors: He didn’t believe robots could be intelligent / He was afraid of getting in trouble with his parents / He thought the robot was too dangerous to handle.
Explanation: The text states Liam hesitated because of the "destruction" around R-7X, implying concern for its safety. The other options are incorrect because the robot is described as intellectual, Liam’s parents were negotiated with later (not initially feared), and there’s no mention of danger—only curiosity.
---
Q2: What does the phrase "a matching personality" suggest about R-7X?
Correct: It shares Liam’s traits, such as curiosity and patience.
Distractors: It mimics Liam’s behavior perfectly / It has a completely different personality / It was programmed to be identical to Liam.
Explanation: The text explains R-7X has a matching personality to Liam’s—meaning similar qualities, not an exact copy or a programmed duplicate. "Mimics perfectly" and "programmed identical" are too absolute and unsupported.
---
Q3: How does Liam’s relationship with R-7X change over time?
Correct: He moves from caution to embracing their friendship.
Distractors: He becomes afraid of the robot / He realizes the robot is too advanced for him / He stops trusting R-7X after it fixes his sister’s toy.
Explanation: The text shows Liam starts with hesitation but eventually embraces their friendship. There’s no fear, doubt about R-7X’s intelligence, or loss of trust—only growing comfort.
---
Q4: What role does R-7X play in resolving the conflict with Liam’s sister?
Correct: It uses its tools to repair her broken toy.
Distractors: It scolds the sister for being careless / It refuses to help because it’s not programmed for emotions / It asks Liam to fix it instead.
Explanation: The text states R-7X resolved the issue by fixing the toy, not by scolding or avoiding the problem. The robot’s ability to help with emotions is later shown, so refusing help is incorrect.
---
Q5: What does the final exchange between Liam and R-7X reveal about their bond?
Correct: They see each other as more than just a machine and a boy.
Distractors: They realize they are the same / They agree to work together only for practical reasons / They decide to hide their friendship from Liam’s parents.
Explanation: The dialogue ("You’re more than just a machine" / "And you’re more than just a boy") highlights their embrace of each other’s deeper nature. The other options contradict the text—there’s no claim of sameness, no mention of hiding the friendship, or practical-only cooperation.



Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of being famous

Text:
Fame can open doors, but it also comes with challenges. For many, the potential to inspire or aid others is a powerful motivator. Entrepreneurs like Oprah Winfrey or Elon Musk leverage their fame to fund charities, launch initiatives, or stream ideas to millions. A single flash of recognition can turn a small business into a global brand, offering opportunities for recruitment of top talent. However, fame is not without risks. The pressure to maintain a perfect image can lead to destruction of personal relationships or mental health. Public scrutiny may force individuals to suspend their private lives, while hackers might exploit leaked passwords to damage reputations. Even a single misstep—like a poorly timed tweet—can spiral into a crisis. Balancing success and stability requires careful navigation, proving that fame, while rewarding, demands resilience.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text suggest about the role of fame in business?
Correct: It can accelerate growth by attracting opportunities.
Distractors: It guarantees long-term financial security / It always leads to ethical dilemmas / It replaces the need for hard work.
Explanation: The text states that fame can turn a small business into a global brand ("A single flash of recognition can turn a small business into a global brand"), implying growth opportunities. It does not claim fame guarantees security ("not without risks"), causes ethical dilemmas (not mentioned), or replaces hard work (implied by "demands resilience").
---
Q2: How does the author describe the risks of fame?
Correct: They can damage personal well-being and privacy.
Distractors: They are easily avoidable with proper planning / They only affect public figures / They primarily involve legal consequences.
Explanation: The text mentions "pressure to maintain a perfect image can lead to destruction of personal relationships or mental health" and "public scrutiny may force individuals to suspend their private lives," linking fame to well-being and privacy. The risks are not described as avoidable ("demands resilience"), limited to public figures (implies broader impact), or primarily legal (no mention of laws).
---
Q3: What is the meaning of "a single misstep" in the context?
Correct: A minor error with serious consequences.
Distractors: A deliberate attempt to gain attention / A planned strategy to test public reaction / A temporary setback with no lasting effects.
Explanation: The text uses "misstep" to describe a "poorly timed tweet" that "can spiral into a crisis," meaning an unintentional mistake with major repercussions. It is not deliberate ("misstep" implies unintentional), a strategy (no planning implied), or temporary ("spiral into a crisis" suggests lasting damage).
---
Q4: Why does the author mention "passwords" in the paragraph?
Correct: To illustrate how fame increases vulnerability to cyber threats.
Distractors: To explain how celebrities protect their digital privacy / To argue that fame requires complex security systems / To suggest that hackers target only famous people.
Explanation: The text states "hackers might exploit leaked passwords to damage reputations," linking fame to cyber risks. It does not discuss privacy protection (no mention of safeguards), security systems (not the focus), or exclusive targeting ("can" implies potential risk, not certainty).
---
Q5: What is the overall message of the essay?
Correct: Fame offers advantages but requires careful management to avoid pitfalls.
Distractors: Fame is always beneficial if used responsibly / Fame destroys personal lives regardless of efforts / Fame should be avoided due to its inherent dangers.
Explanation: The text balances benefits ("open doors," "leverage fame to aid others") with warnings ("risks," "destruction," "demands resilience"), emphasizing the need for balance. It does not claim fame is always beneficial ("not without risks"), that it always destroys lives ("requires resilience"), or that it should be avoided (no absolute condemnation).



Topic: an article about gardening without soil

Text:
Gardening without soil, or hydroponics, has gained considerable popularity among urban residents who lack space for traditional gardens. This democratic approach allows even apartment dwellers to grow fresh herbs, vegetables, and flowers using nutrient-rich water instead of earth. Beyond personal satisfaction, hydroponic gardens can also attract wildlife, such as bees and butterflies, creating a mini ecosystem on balconies or rooftops.

One innovative way to integrate hydroponics into city life is through community projects. For example, a spectator sports stadium in Tokyo recently installed a hydroponic farm along its perimeter, generating revenue from selling produce to local restaurants. The project also lowered food transportation costs and reduced the city’s carbon footprint. Critics argue that such systems require lower maintenance than soil-based gardens, but enthusiasts point out that they can be more efficient in water use.

Another trend is the fusion of hydroponics with gaming culture. Virtual farming simulations, like those in mobile apps, teach players the basics of hydroponic systems before they try real-world setups. Some educators even use these games to spark interest in sustainable agriculture among students. While skeptics cry foul over the commercialization of gardening, supporters argue that technology makes this method accessible to everyone.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text suggest about the appeal of hydroponics to urban residents?
Correct: It offers a practical solution for those with limited space.
Distractors: It is primarily used for commercial farming / It requires more land than traditional gardening / It is only popular among professional gardeners.
Explanation: The text states that hydroponics is popular among urban residents who "lack space for traditional gardens," making it a practical solution. It does not mention commercial farming as the primary use, nor does it suggest it requires more land—hydroponics is designed for small spaces. The idea that it is only popular among professionals is contradicted by its accessibility to apartment dwellers and gamers.
---
Q2: How does the Tokyo stadium’s hydroponic project benefit the city?
Correct: It reduces food transportation costs and carbon emissions.
Distractors: It increases the stadium’s ticket revenue / It eliminates the need for local restaurants / It makes the stadium more attractive to spectators by offering free produce.
Explanation: The text explicitly states that the project "lowered food transportation costs and reduced the city’s carbon footprint." While the stadium generates revenue from selling produce, this is not the primary environmental benefit mentioned. There is no evidence that it eliminates the need for local restaurants or that free produce attracts spectators—the focus is on sustainability.
---
Q3: Why might some people criticize the commercialization of hydroponics?
Correct: They believe it overshadows the traditional, hands-on nature of gardening.
Distractors: They argue that hydroponic systems are too expensive for most people / They claim that virtual gaming makes hydroponics less effective / They insist that hydroponics cannot support wildlife.
Explanation: The text mentions that skeptics "cry foul over the commercialization of gardening," implying concern about losing the authentic, democratic spirit of gardening. There is no mention of hydroponics being too expensive, nor is there evidence that virtual gaming reduces effectiveness—it is used as an educational tool. The text actually states that hydroponics can attract wildlife, so the last option is incorrect.
---
Q4: How does the text describe the maintenance requirements of hydroponic systems compared to soil-based gardens?
Correct: They require lower maintenance.
Distractors: They demand more frequent watering / They are equally labor-intensive / They need specialized equipment that most people cannot afford.
Explanation: The text explicitly states that hydroponic systems require "lower maintenance than soil-based gardens." The other options contradict this: soil gardens typically require more frequent watering, and while hydroponics may need some equipment, the text does not suggest it is unaffordable or equally labor-intensive.
---
Q5: What role does gaming play in promoting hydroponics, according to the text?
Correct: It serves as an educational tool to teach people about hydroponic systems.
Distractors: It is used to generate revenue for hydroponic companies / It replaces real-world gardening for many enthusiasts / It discourages people from trying hydroponics in practice.
Explanation: The text explains that virtual farming simulations "teach players the basics of hydroponic systems before they try real-world setups," making gaming an educational tool. There is no mention of revenue generation from games, nor does the text suggest that gaming replaces real gardening—it complements it. The idea that it discourages people is unsupported by the text, which highlights its accessibility.
